NOTICE AND AGENDA
11555 W. Civic Center Drive, Marana, Arizona 85653
Council Chambers, January 6, 2026, at or after 6:00 PM
Jon Post, Mayor

Pursuant to A.R.S. §38-431.02, notice is hereby given to the members of the Marana
Town Council and to the general public that the Town Council will hold a meeting open
to the public on January 6, 2026, at or after 6:00 PM located in the Council Chambers of
the Ed Honea Marana Municipal Complex, 11555 W. Civic Center Drive, Marana,
Arizona. If the Council Chambers reaches capacity, overflow seating with a live stream of
the meeting will be available at the Marana Police Department Community Room.  
ACTION MAY BE TAKEN BY THE COUNCIL ON ANY ITEM LISTED ON THIS
AGENDA. Revisions to the agenda can occur up to 24 hours prior to the meeting.
Revisions to the agenda appear in italics.
 
As a courtesy to others, please turn off or put in silent mode all electronic devices.
Meeting Times
Welcome to this Marana Town Council meeting. Regular Council meetings are usually
held the first and third Tuesday of each month at 6:00 PM at the Ed Honea Marana
Municipal Complex, although the date or time may change and additional meetings may
be called at other times and/or places. Contact the Town Clerk or watch for posted
agendas for other meetings. This agenda may be revised up to 24 hours prior to the
meeting. In such a case a new agenda will be posted in place of this agenda. Public access
to the Council Chambers begins 20 minutes prior to the start of the meeting.
Speaking at Meetings
Speaking at Meetings
If you are interested in speaking to the Council during the Call to the Public or Public
Hearings, you must fill out a speaker card and deliver it to the Town Clerk.
All persons attending the Council meeting, whether speaking to the Council or not, are
expected to observe the Council rules, as well as the rules of politeness, propriety,
decorum and good conduct. Any person interfering with the meeting in any way, or
acting rudely or loudly will be removed from the meeting and will not be allowed to
return.
Accessibility
To better serve the citizens of Marana and others attending our meetings, the Council
Chambers are wheelchair and handicapped accessible. Persons with a disability may
request a reasonable accommodation, such as a sign language interpreter, by contacting
the Town Clerk at (520) 382-1999. Requests should be made as early as possible to
arrange the accommodation.
Agendas
Copies of the agenda are available the day of the meeting in the lobby outside the
Council Chambers or online at www.maranaaz.gov under Agendas and Minutes. For
questions about the Council meetings, special services or procedures, please contact the
Town Clerk, at (520) 382-1999, Monday through Friday from 8:00 AM to 5:00 PM.
This Notice and Agenda Posted no later than 24 hours prior to the meeting, at the Ed
Honea Marana Municipal Complex, 11555 W. Civic Center Drive, the Marana Operations
Center, 5100 W. Ina Road, and at www.maranaaz.gov under Agendas and Minutes.
 
REGULAR COUNCIL MEETING
           
CALL TO ORDER AND ROLL CALL
 
PLEDGE OF ALLEGIANCE/INVOCATION/MOMENT OF SILENCE
 
APPROVAL OF AGENDA
 
CALL TO THE PUBLIC
At this time any member of the public is allowed to address the Town Council on any
issue within the jurisdiction of the Town Council, except for items scheduled for a Public
Hearing at this meeting. The speaker may have up to three minutes to speak. Any
persons wishing to address the Council must complete a speaker card located outside
the Council Chambers and deliver it to the Town Clerk prior to the commencement of
the meeting. Individuals addressing a meeting at the Call to the Public will not be
provided with electronic technology capabilities beyond the existing voice amplification
and recording capabilities in the facilities. Pursuant to the Arizona Open Meeting Law, at
the conclusion of Call to the Public, individual members of the Council may respond to
the conclusion of Call to the Public, individual members of the Council may respond to
criticism made by those who have addressed the Council, and may ask staff to review
the matter, or may ask that the matter be placed on a future agenda.
 
PROCLAMATIONS
 
MAYOR AND COUNCIL REPORTS: SUMMARY OF CURRENT EVENTS
 
MANAGER’S REPORT: SUMMARY OF CURRENT EVENTS
 
PRESENTATIONS
 
CONSENT AGENDA
The Consent Agenda contains items requiring action by the Council which are generally
routine items not requiring Council discussion. A single motion and affirmative vote will
approve all items on the Consent Agenda, including any resolutions or ordinances. Prior
to a motion to approve the Consent Agenda, any Council member may remove any item
from the Consent Agenda and that item will be discussed and voted upon separately.

A.R.S. §38-431.03 (A). 
 
FUTURE AGENDA ITEMS
Notwithstanding the Mayor’s discretion regarding the items to be placed on the agenda,
if three or more Council members request that an item be placed on the agenda, it must
be placed on the agenda for the second regular Town Council meeting after the date of
the request, pursuant to Marana Town Code Section 2-4-2(B).

Subject: Resolution No. 2026-001: Relating to Budget and Procurement:
approving a change in budget authority for the Tangerine TI
Improvements (ST107) project; approving the transfer of up to
$1,500,000 in budgeted expenditure authority from Other Agency
Funding (Fund 3045) to Northeast Impact fees (fund 3066) and the
transfer of up to $2,259,327 in budgeted expenditure authority from
Other Agency Funding (fund 3045) to Transportation funds (Fund
3030); approving change orders to the contract with Borderland
Construction Inc. for the Tangerine TI Improvements (ST107) project in
the amount of $9,065,553.33 for the construction of the project and to
construct a driveway entrance for Cottonwood Properties; authorizing
the transfer of appropriations if necessary for this change in budget
authority and change orders; and authorizing the Town Manager or
designee to execute the necessary documents to effectuate the change
in budget authority and change orders (Fausto Burruel)
 
Discussion:
The budget for fiscal year 2025-2026 was adopted on June 17, 2025, which set the legal
level of budgetary control at the department level for the General Fund and at the fund
level for all other funds. Council approval is required for budgetary transfers between
General Fund departments, between funds, and for any transfer of contingency. The
Council-approved budget also includes funding for the Town’s five-year CIP program.
The Tangerine TI Improvements, Project No. ST107 (the "Project") was properly
included within the Town of Marana's FY2026 Capital Improvement Plan. 
The Project was originally programmed in the fiscal year 2026 budget with $4,059,327
of Other Agency Funding (fund 3045) to allow the expenditure of funds from outside
agencies. To align the project funding with the Town’s previously adopted
Infrastructure Improvement Plan, Town staff is requesting that $1,500,000 in budget
authority of Other Agency Funding be reprogrammed as Northeast Impact Fees (Fund
3066) and $2,259,327 in budget authority of Other Agency Funding will need to be
reprogrammed as Transportation funds (Fund 3030) to provide funding needed to
deliver the project timely.  Staff are working to calculate the reimbursable portion of
Transportation funds from current and/or future impact fees. The remaining $300,000
in Other Agency Funding will remain to fund a driveway entrance for Cottonwood
Properties and other potential private improvements that may be requested.
Marana Town Code ("MTC") Section 3-4-7(B)(3) provides that Town Council approval
is necessary for change orders that individually or cumulatively exceed the amounts set
forth in MTC 3-4-8(A)(3).
The Town has received a Guaranteed Maximum Price (GMP) bid to construct the
Project from Borderland Construction in the amount of $8,976,829.77. This will be GMP
18 and will require council approval as stated in the Marana Town Code ("MTC"),
Section 3-4-7(B)(3).
The Town has received a request to construct a private driveway on the project from
Cottonwood Properties. The cost to construct this driveway is $88,723.56. The cost to
construct this driveway will be paid for by Cottonwood Properties. This will be GMP
19 and will require council approval as stated in the Marana Town Code ("MTC"),
Section 3-4-7(B)(3).
The total of these two change orders is $9,065,553.33.
Financial Impact:
Fiscal Year: 2026
Budgeted
Y/N:
Yes
Amount: $3,759,327
Transferring $1,500,000 from Other Agency Funding (Fund 3045) to Northeast Impact
fees (fund 3066).
Transferring $2,259,327 from Other Agency Funding (fund 3045) to Transportation
funds (Fund 3030).
As this is a reallocation of budget authority there is no impact to the overall Town
budget.
Further, the costs for GMP18 were budgeted as part of the FY 2026 budget and the cost
of the driveway will be paid by a third party.
 
Staff Recommendation:
Staff recommends approval of Resolution No. 2026-001.  
Suggested Motion:
I move to adopt Resolution No. 2026-001, approving a change in budget authority for
the Tangerine TI Improvements (ST107) project; approving the transfer of up to
$1,500,000 in budgeted expenditure authority from Other Agency Funding (Fund 3045)
to Northeast Impact fees (fund 3066) and the transfer of up to $2,259,327 in budgeted
expenditure authority from Other Agency Funding (fund 3045) to Transportation funds
(Fund 3030); approving change orders to the contract with Borderland Construction
Inc. for the Tangerine TI Improvements (ST107) project in the amount of $9,065,553.33
for the construction of the project and to construct a driveway entrance for
Cottonwood Properties; authorizing the transfer of appropriations if necessary for this
change in budget authority and change orders; and authorizing the Town Manager or
designee to execute the necessary documents to effectuate the change in budget
authority and change orders.

Council-Regular Meeting   C2        
Meeting Date: 01/06/2026  
To: Mayor and Council
Submitted For: Jane Fairall, Town Attorney 
From: Reyna Celis, Senior Legal Assistant
Date: January 6, 2026
Strategic Plan Focus Area:
Not Applicable 
Subject: Resolution No. 2026-002: Relating to Public Works; approving and
authorizing the Mayor to sign a public improvement participation
agreement with R11 Tangerine Corporation for the construction of
driveway improvements as part of the Tangerine Road Traffic
Interchange (TI) Improvement Project No. ST107 (Jane Fairall)
Discussion:
R11 Tangerine Corporation ("R11"), an affiliate of Cottonwood Properties, Inc., plans to
develop Lot 6 of the Final Plat for Tangerine/I-10 Lots 1-10, Parcel A, recorded in the
Pima County Recorder’s Office at Sequence No. 20210990141, located within the
Tangerine Commerce Park Specific Plan on the south side of Tangerine Road, west of
the Interstate 10 and Tangerine Road traffic interchange (the “Subject Property”). R11
will be developing the Subject Property for various commercial uses and needs a
driveway within the eastbound dual right turn bay on Tangerine Road (the “Driveway
Improvements”). The Town of Marana has selected Borderland Construction
Company, Inc. (“Borderland”) as the Construction Manager at Risk (“CMAR”) for the
Tangerine Road Traffic Interchange Project, Town of Marana Project No. ST107 (the
“Road Project”).  
R11 has requested that the Town build the Driveway Improvements as a part of its
Road Project, with R11 being responsible for all costs. The proposed public
improvement participation agreement ("PIPA") provides that R11 shall deposit with
the Town the full anticipated costs of construction of the Driveway Improvements
($88,723.56) in order for the Town to complete the Driveway Improvements for
R11.  No later than 90 days after substantial completion of the Driveway
Improvements, the Town will provide to R11 an itemized statement of the Town's
construction and design costs for the Driveway Improvements and a check or invoice
representing the difference between Marana’s costs and R11's cash deposit. If R11's
cash deposit was insufficient to cover R11's obligations under the agreement, R11 will
pay Marana the balance of the costs for the Driveway Improvements as set forth in the
invoice within 30 days after the itemized statement and invoice are provided to R11.
Any amounts unpaid after 30 days shall accrue simple interest at the rate of 10% per
year until paid in full.
Financial Impact:
No financial impact to the Town is anticipated due to this agreement, as R11 Tangerine
Corporation will pay for all costs associated with the Driveway Improvements.
Staff Recommendation:
Staff recommends approval of the agreement.
Suggested Motion:
I move to adopt Resolution No. 2026-002, approving and authorizing the Mayor to sign
a public improvement participation agreement with R11 Tangerine Corporation for the
construction of driveway improvements as part of the Tangerine Road Traffic
Interchange (TI) Improvement Project No. ST107.

“Road Project”); and

established by the December 20, 2005 adoption of Marana Ordinance No. 2005.27, recorded

subdivision thereof by R11 and the Owner; and
WHEREAS R11 will be developing the Subject Property for various commercial
uses and needs a driveway within the eastbound dual right turn bay on Tangerine Road
(the “Driveway Improvements”); and

Road Project; and

R11/MARANA TANGERINE ROAD PIPA 
-1 -
P
UBLIC IMPROVEMENT PARTICIPATION AGREEMENT 
TO
WN OF MARANA, ARIZONA 
This Public Improvement Participation Agreement (this “Agreement”) is made and 
entered into by and between R11 TANGERINE CORPORATION, an Arizona corporation (“R11”), and 
th
e TOWN OF MARANA, an Arizona municipal corporation (“Marana”). R11 and Marana are 
so
metimes collectively referred to in this Agreement as the “Parties,” either of which is sometimes 
referred to as a “Party.” 
RECITALS 
A.Marana has entered into a Construction Manager at Risk (“CMAR”) contract with
BORDERLAND CONSTRUCTION COMPANY, INC. (“Borderland”) for the construction of the Tangerine 
Ro
ad Traffic Interchange (TI) improvements, Town of Marana Project No. ST107 (the “Road 
Project”). 
B.Tangerine/I-10 LLC (“Owner”) previously purchased all of  the commercial property
located within the Tangerine Commerce Park Specific Plan established by the December 20, 2005 
adoption of Marana Ordinance No. 2005.27, recorded in the Pima County Recorder’s Office at 
Docket 2706, Page 1559 (Sequence No. 20052470427), generally located on the south side of 
Tangerine Road, west of the Interstate 10 and Tangerine Road traffic interchange.  
C.Lot 6 as identified on the Final Plat for Tangerine/I-10 Lots 1-10, Parcel A, recorded in
the Pima County Recorder’s Office at Sequence No. 20210990141 is owned by the Owner and R11 
has entered into an agreement to purchase the northern portion of Lot 6 along the Tangerine Road 
frontage (“Subject Property”) upon subdivision thereof by R11 and the Owner. 
D.R11 will  be developing  the Subject Property for various commercial uses and needs a
driveway within the eastbound dual right turn bay on Tangerine Road (the “Driveway 
Improvements”). 
E.The Parties believe that the most economical and least disruptive way to accomplish the
prompt Driveway Improvements for and on the Subject Property is for the Parties to arrange to 
have the work completed by Borderland in connection with the construction of the Road Project. 
F.Borderland has provided the Parties with a proposed price of $88,723.56 for the Driveway
Improvements.  A true and correct copy of Borderland’s proposal is attached to and incorporated 
in this Agreement as Exhibit A. 
G.The purpose of this Agreement is to facilitate Marana’s completion of the Driveway
Improvements for and on the Subject Property as part of the Road Project with funding provided 
by R11. 
AGREEMENT 
NOW, THEREFORE, based on the foregoing recitals, which are incorporated here as the 
in
tention of the Parties in entering into this Agreement, the Parties agree as follows: 
Exhibit A to Marana Resolution No. 2026-002
R11/MARANA TANGERINE ROAD PIPA 
-2 -
1
. Depiction of the Driveway Improvements for and on the Subject Property.  The
Driveway Improvements depiction attached as Exhibit B shows the nature and extent of the work 
to be completed by Borderland pursuant to this Agreement, which Marana and Borderland shall 
incorporate into the Road Project design. 
2. Grant of right of entry and temporary construction easement. Owner and R11 hereby
grant Marana and Borderland a right of entry and temporary construction easement over the 
Subject Property, to accommodate completion of the Driveway Improvements. Owner joins in this 
Agreement for the purpose of granting such easement as evidenced by Owner’s signature below. 
3. R11’s obligation to pay costs for the Driveway Improvements as described in Exhibit
A.R11 shall pay all costs associated with the completion of the Driveway Improvements for and on
the Subject Property. Marana and R11 agree that the construction costs for the Driveway
Improvements are anticipated to be $88,723.56, as itemized and attached to this Agreement as
Exhibit A.
4. Cash deposit to secure R11’s obligations under this Agreement. R11’s obligations under
this Agreement, including without limitation R11’s obligations under paragraph 3 above, shall be 
secured by a cash deposit to Marana in the amount of $88,723.56, referred to in this Agreement as 
“R11’s Cash Deposit.” R11 shall provide R11’s Cash Deposit to the Town within 15 business days 
after the Effective Date of this Agreement. 
5. Reconciliation and final payment. No later than 90 days after substantial completion of
the Driveway Improvements for and on the Subject Property, Marana shall provide to R11 an 
itemized statement of Marana’s construction and design costs for the Driveway Improvements and 
a check or invoice representing the difference between Marana’s costs and R11’s Cash Deposit. If 
R11’s Cash Deposit was insufficient to cover R11’s obligations under this Agreement, R11 shall pay 
Marana the balance of the costs for the Driveway Improvements for and on the Subject Property as 
set forth in the invoice within 30 days after the itemized statement and invoice are provided to R11. 
Any amounts unpaid after 30 days shall accrue simple interest at the rate of 10% per year until paid 
in full. 
6. Recording. This Agreement shall not be recorded.
7. Notices. All notices shall be in writing and shall be sufficiently given and served upon
the other Party by electronic mail, receipt of which has been acknowledged by the other Party, or 
by hand delivery, first class mail, registered or certified, return receipt requested, postage prepaid, 
or by reputable overnight courier service and addressed as follows:

Council-Regular Meeting   C3

To: Mayor and Council

From: George Cardieri, Real Property Manager
Date: January 6, 2026
Strategic Plan Focus Area:
Not Applicable 
Subject: Resolution No. 2026-003: Relating to Real Estate; vacating public
right-of-way located south of West Massingale Road and east of North
Hartman Lane in Section 36, Township 12 South, Range 12 East (George
Cardieri)
Discussion:
Town staff have identified a triangular-shaped area designated as public right-of-way,
located south of West Massingale Road and east of North Hartman Lane, that lies
entirely within a privately owned parcel (APN: 226-35-004B) owned by Mr. Bults, Inc.,
an Illinois corporation. The area is approximately 5,811 square feet and was originally
intended for use by Pima County for floodplain management related to a project that
ultimately was not constructed. The subject public right-of-way and surrounding
property were subsequently annexed into the Town of Marana on April 16, 1991,
through adoption of Ordinance No. 91.12, recorded in the office of the Pima County
Recorder on June 25, 1991, at Docket 9067, Page 623. Since then, the property has
retained its designation as "right-of-way" but is not needed by the Town for
right-of-way uses now or in the future. The Town is authorized by A.R.S.
§§ 9-240(B)(3)(e) and 28-7205 to vacate and abandon unneeded Town rights-of-way and
roadways. Approval of the proposed resolution will abandon the right-of-way and
authorize the Town Engineer to sign a Quit Claim Deed of Abandonment,
quit-claiming the right-of-way to Mr. Bult’s, Inc., the current owner of the adjacent land.
Staff Recommendation:
Staff recommends approval of the abandonment.
Suggested Motion:
I move to adopt Resolution No. 2026-003, vacating public right-of-way located south of
West Massingale Road and east of North Hartman Lane in Section 36, Township 12
South, Range 12 East.
Attachments
Resolution No. 2026-003 
Exhibit A (Legal Description) 
Exhibit B (Quit Claim Deed of Abandonment)

Resolution No. 2026-003 1
MARANA RESOLUTION NO. 2026-003
RELATING TO REAL ESTATE; VACATING PUBLIC RIGHT-OF-WAY LOCATED
SOUTH OF WEST MASSINGALE ROAD AND EAST OF NORTH HARTMAN LANE IN
SECTION 36, TOWNSHIP 12 SOUTH, RANGE 12 EAST
WHEREAS, on April 16, 1991, the Town of Marana passed and adopted Ordinance
No. 91.12, recorded in the office of the Pima County Recorder on June 25, 1991, at Docket
9067 Page 623, approving an annexation of property to the Town of Marana (the
“Lawyer’s Title Annexation”); and
WHEREAS the Lawyer’s Title Annexation included property that had been
established in Pima County as right-of-way, including approximately 5,811 square feet of
right-of-way located south of West Massingale Road and east of North Hartman Lane in
Section 36, Township 12 South, Range 12 East (the “Subject Right-of-Way”); and
WHEREAS the Subject Right-of-Way was originally intended to be used by Pima
County for floodplain management for a project that ultimately was not constructed; and
WHEREAS the Town of Marana is authorized by A.R.S. §§ 9-240 (B)(3)(e) and
28-7205 to vacate and abandon Town rights-of-way and roadways; and
WHEREAS the Town Council finds that the value of the Subject Right-of-Way is
nominal and that vacating it is in the best interests of the Town and its citizens.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED BY THE MAYOR AND COUNCIL OF
THE TOWN OF MARANA, ARIZONA, AS FOLLOWS:
SECTION 1. The Town of Marana hereby vacates and abandons the Subject Right-
of-Way more particularly described in Exhibit A attached to and incorporated within this
resolution by this reference.
SECTION 2. The Town Engineer is hereby authorized to sign the Quit Claim Deed
of Abandonment in substantially the form attached to and incorporated within this
resolution by this reference as Exhibit B, quit-claiming the Subject Right-of-Way to Mr.
Bult’s, Inc., the current owner of the adjacent land.
SECTION 3. The Town’s Manager and staff are hereby directed and authorized to
undertake all other and further tasks required or beneficial to carry out this resolution.
Resolution No. 2026-003 2
PASSED AND ADOPTED by the Mayor and Council of the Town of Marana,
Arizona, this 6th day of January, 2026.

Meeting Date: 01/06/2026  
To: Mayor and Council

From: Dan Grossman, CIP Process Analyst
Date: January 6, 2026
Strategic Plan Focus Area:

Strategic Plan Focus Area Additional Info:
  
Subject: Relating to Procurement; approving a change order to the contract
with Psomas, Inc. in the amount of $181,580.93 for the Tangerine Road
Widening Phase 2A Project (ST098); authorizing the transfer of
appropriations, if necessary, for the change order; and authorizing the
Town Manager or designee to execute the necessary documents to
effectuate the change order (Fausto Burruel)
Discussion:
Marana Town Code ("MTC") Section 3-4-7(B)(3) provides that Town Council approval
is necessary for change orders that individually or cumulatively exceed the amounts set
forth in MTC Section 3-4-8(A)(3).
The budget for fiscal year 2025-2026 was adopted on June 17, 2025 and included the
Town’s five-year CIP program. The Tangerine Road Widening, Phase 2A, Project No.
ST098 (the "Project") was properly included within the Town of Marana's FY2026
Capital Improvement Plan.
Effective January 31, 2022, the Town entered into a professional services agreement
with Psomas, Inc. ("Consultant") for the design of Tangerine 2A in the amount of
$3,500,548 ("Agreement"). Since then, several amendments have been executed
involving change orders (a summary of which can be reviewed in the staff report
accompanying the agenda materials for the November 18, 2025 Town Council
Meeting). Some of the amendments involved archaeological discoveries that were
made on the site, as follows: 
Effective February 8, 2024, the Town executed an amendment to the Agreement
adding Phase II data recovery to the scope of work and increasing the Consultant's
fee in the amount of $349,695.00 to account for the added work.
Effective August 5, 2024, the Town executed an amendment to the Agreement
adding Phase II data recovery to the scope of work and increasing the Consultant's
fee in the amount of $381,786.75 to account for the added work.
Effective September 23, 2024, the Town executed an amendment to the Agreement
adding Phase II data recovery to the scope of work and increasing the Consultant's
fee in the amount of $282,242.50 to account for the added work.
Effective February 17, 2025, the Town executed an amendment to the Agreement
adding Phase II data recovery to the scope of work and increasing the Consultant's
fee in the amount of $561,272.00to account for the added work.
While performing the Phase II data recovery, the consultant has continued to encounter
cultural resources and funeral remains that require additional data recovery efforts. A
change order is required in the amount of $181,580.93 for this additional work.
The total fee for the Consultant for Phase II data recovery will increase to $1,756,577.68.
 
Financial Impact:
Fiscal Year: 2026
Budgeted
Y/N:
Yes
Amount: $181,580.93
This change order will be funded with Northeast Impact Fees, GL Account #
36643000-8025-ST098.
 
Staff Recommendation:
Staff recommends approval of the change order to the Psomas Inc. Professional Service
Agreement for the Tangerine Rd Widening Phase 2A, ST098 project in the amount of
$181,580.93.
 
Suggested Motion:
I move to approve the change order to the contract with Psomas, Inc. in the amount of
I move to approve the change order to the contract with Psomas, Inc. in the amount of

Manager or designee to execute the necessary documents to effectuate the change

data recovery.

with you, it is anticipated that a modification to the existing contract will be issued for this work.

Attachments: Westland Change Order Scope of Work an d Fee Proposal 
   
https://api.box.com/wopi/files/2066528948718/WOPIServiceId_TP_BOX_2/WOPIUserId_45835045683/756_14_Change Order X_Phase II Data Recovery.docx Change Order | Page 1 of 3 
CHANGE ORDER 
Change Order No.: 10  
Name of Project: Continued Cultural Resource Services for the Tangerine Road Corridor 
Project (Interstate 10 to Dove Mountain Boulevard/Twin Peaks Road) 
Project No.: 756.14 
Date:  December 8, 2025 
 
WestLand Resources, Inc., an Arizona corporation, d/b/a WestLand Engineering & Environmental Services 
(“WestLand”) is providing this contract modification to the Agreement that was fully executed on March 30, 
2022 by WestLand and PSOMAS. 
1. CHANGES TO SCOPE OF SERVICES. 
The Scope of Services is changed as follows: 
WestLand initiated Phase II data recovery investigations at AZ AA:12:422(ASM) on February 26, 2024.  
To date, WestLand has completed Phase II excavations and Phase III construction monitoring in support 
of the project.  During Phase II excavations, WestLand documented and excavated a total of 382 
archaeological features and fully recovered a total of 198 mortuary features and isolated human remains. 
Change Order No. 9 assumed a total of 177 mortuary features would be recovered and documented. This 
is 22 more mortuary features than originally assumed in the previous proposal and subsequent change 
orders. The original proposal and subsequent change orders stated that a fee of $3,500.00 would be 
assessed for the excavation, analysis, reporting, and repatriation in the event that additional ancestral 
burials are encountered. For these 22 additional mortuary features, the $3,500.00 fee per burial totals  
$73,500.00.  
 
Overall, the data recovery work at AZ AA:12:422(ASM) contained a higher than anticipated number of 
habitation structures, mortuary features, deeper sediment deposition, and a higher number of artifacts. This 
has led to increased levels of effort in the post-fieldwork tasks that remain to be completed for the project. 
To date, WestLand’s cultural resources team has drafted three end- of-fieldwork reports to support and 
expedite construction clearance for the project. This was two more end-of-fieldwork reports than originally 
assumed in the previous proposals and change orders.  
 
In November 2024, WestLand repatriated the first set of mortuary features to the Tohono O’odham Nation 
based on the Nation’s  traditional cultural practices. WestLand subsequently drafted the first Report of 
Remains, a permit obligation that documents  the mortuary features that have been repatriated to the 
Nation. In November 2025, WestLand transported all of the remaining mortuary features to the Nation 
outside of Sells, Arizona. WestLand is actively working on the second Report of Remains that documents 
the final repatriation event for the project. This was one more report than originally assumed in the 
previous proposals and change orders.     
   
WestLand’s cultural resources team is also actively working on the artifact analysis, the draft data recovery 
report, and curation tasks that remain to be completed for the project.  Due to the extent of the Phase II 
excavation work, more artifacts , specialized samples,  and faunal specimens were recovered than 
originally anticipated. This has led to an increase in labor efforts by our analysts , report authors, and  
laboratory staff conducting the documentation, reporting, and curation work.       
 
WestLand will continue to complete the following tasks for the project: 
 
1. Laboratory Processing. Additional labor hours to complete the lab processing to state standards 
for archaeological data recovery projects.  
 
https://api.box.com/wopi/files/2066528948718/WOPIServiceId_TP_BOX_2/WOPIUserId_45835045683/756_14_Change Order X_Phase II Data Recovery.docx Change Order | Page 2 of 3 
2. Analysis. Additional labor hours to complete the documentation and analysis required by the ASM for 
archaeological data recovery projects.   
3. Report. Additional labor hours to draft a draft data recovery report and the second report of remains to 
current State Historic Preservation Office (SHPO)  and ASM standards. A draft archaeological 
report will be submitted within 12 months of the end of fieldwork, and the Report of Remains must 
be submitted within 120 days of repatriation. It is anticipated that the draft data recovery report 
will be submitted in May 2026 and the second report of remains will be submitted in March 2026.  
4. Curation. The artifacts and samples recovered during the phased data recovery program will be 
curated with the ASM along with all field notes, photographs, and reports. Given the size of the 
artifact assemblage, additional labor hours will be needed to prepare the collection for curation in 
perpetuity at the ASM. The Project’s collection must meet the Museum’s curation standards which 
includes multiple reviews prior to Museum’s acceptance of the collection. The final estimated 
ASM project registration and curation fee for the project is $134,194.73.  
 
 
2. COST(S). 
As of December  2025, the remaining project funds total $84,343.00. The final estimated ASM project 
registration and curation fee for the project is $134,194.73. WestLand’s estimate, to the best of our ability 
and in good faith, of the total cost of the Services added by this Change Order is $181,580.93. A breakdown 
of costs is provided in Table 1.

MARANA TOWN COUNCIL

11555 W. Civic Center Drive, Marana, Arizona 85653

SUMMARY MINUTES 
 
CALL TO ORDER AND ROLL CALL 
 
Mayor Post called the meeting to order  at 6:01 PM and directed the Town Clerk to call 
the roll. Council Member Comerford  was absent. All other Council Members were 
present, constituting a quorum. 
 
PLEDGE OF ALLEGIANCE/INVOCATION/MOMENT OF SILENCE:  Led by Mayor 
Post.  
 
APPROVAL OF AGENDA 
 
Council Member  Kai moved to approve the agenda as presented. Vice Mayor Ziegler 
seconded the motion. Motion passed, 6-0. 
  
DISCUSSION/DIRECTION/POSSIBLE ACTION  
 
D1     Relating to Wastewater: Presentation and discussion on the Water Reclamation 
Master Plan Update by HDR, Inc. (Mike Osborne)  
 

 
Council Study Session Meeting 
December 9, 2025 
 
Page 2 of 3 
 
Water Director Heidi Lashman introduced the agenda item  and provided a brief 
background on its history. She encouraged the Council to ask questions and introduced 
the HDR Senior Project Manager Eric Hielema.  
 
HDR Water/Wastewater Engineer Hadley Habeck presented an update to the Town’s 
sewer master plan for the Town’s designated management area . A copy of the 
presentation slides is on file with the Town Clerk’s Office.  
 
Ms. Habeck summarized existing system conditions, projected future wastewater flows, 
recommended facility and infrastructure improvements, and a phased implementation 
strategy. She stated that, based on planning for 13% growth, a new treatment plant 
referred to as the “North WRF” would need to open in 2034, an expansion to the existing 
treatment plant would need to be completed in 2037, and a new treatment plant referred 
to as the “South WRF” would need to open in 2041. 
 
Throughout the presentation, Council Members asked clarifying q uestions. Some of the 
topics covered in the questions included how basins were defined within the plan, the 
size of the designated management area , growth projections, locations for treatment 
facilities, odor control, funding, long-term storage credits, and the use of impact fees. 
 
Mayor Post  and Council Members thanked staff and the HDR representatives for the 
presentation.  
 
EXECUTIVE SESSIONS  
 
Pursuant to A.R.S. § 38-431.03, the Town Council may vote to go into executive session, 
which will not be open to the public, to discuss certain matters.  
 
E1 Executive Session pursuant to A.R.S. §38- 431.03 (A), Council may ask for 
discussion or consideration, or consultation with designated Town representatives, or 
consultation for legal advice with the Town Attorney, concerning any matter listed on 
this agenda for any of the reasons listed in A.R.S. §38-431.03 (A).  
 
FUTURE AGENDA ITEMS  
 
Notwithstanding the Mayor’s discretion regarding the items to be placed on the agenda, 
if three or more Council members request that an item be placed on the agenda, it must 
be placed on the agenda for the second regular Town Council meeting after the date  of 
the request, pursuant to Marana Town Code Section 2-4-2(B). 
 
Mayor Post requested future agenda items. There were no requests for future agenda 
items from Council Members. 
 
Council Study Session Meeting 
December 9, 2025

Members, and Town staff participated in a discussion regarding the Town’s legislative 
priorities and the representatives’ legislative priorities for the upcoming 2026 Legislative 
Session. General topics included data centers, referendum laws, engagement with

municipalities to govern themselves , the history of the R egional Transportation 
Authority, RTA Next, economic development, traffic and transportation infrastructure,  
the Ironwood Forest National Monument, potential uses of a former Marana prison, and 
the upcoming state budget.

Council-Regular Meeting   A1        
Meeting Date: 01/06/2026  
To: Mayor and Council
Submitted For: Heidi Lasham, Water Director 
From: Mike Osborne, Water Deputy Director
Date: January 6, 2026
Strategic Plan Focus Area:
Thriving Commerce,  Proactive Public Services 
Subject: PUBLIC HEARING: Ordinance No. 2026.001: Relating to Utilities;
amending the Town of Marana comprehensive fee schedule by
successively increasing the water base fee, water consumption rates,
groundwater resource/acquisition fee, monthly wastewater service fee,
and wastewater volume charge for all Marana residential and
non-residential water and wastewater customers effective February 6,
2026, January 1, 2027, January 1, 2028, January 1, 2029, and January 1,
2030 (Heidi Lasham)
Resolution No. 2026-004: Relating to Utilities; declaring as a public
record filed with the Town Clerk the amendments adopted by
Ordinance No. 2026.001, amending the Town of Marana
comprehensive fee schedule by successively increasing the water base
fee, water consumption rates, groundwater resource/acquisition fee,
monthly wastewater service fee, and wastewater volume charge for all
Marana residential and non-residential water and wastewater
customers effective February 6, 2026, January 1, 2027, January 1, 2028,
January 1, 2029, and January 1, 2030 (Heidi Lasham)
Discussion:
In the summer of 2024, the Town of Marana Water Department engaged Raftelis to
In the summer of 2024, the Town of Marana Water Department engaged Raftelis to
provide consulting services to conduct a rate analysis for the Town's water and water
reclamation rates and fees to ensure they are meeting cost of service needs for the
upcoming five years of operations. On October 21, 2025, Raftelis and Town staff
presented the preliminary findings of the analysis to Mayor & Council. On November
4, 2025, Mayor and Council adopted a notice of intention to increase water and water
reclamation rates, rate components, fees, and service charges over a multi-year
(2026-2030) period; and set the date of tonight's public hearing on the proposed
increases in order to comply with A.R.S. § 9-511.01. 
Based on the analysis performed by Raftelis, Town staff is proposing to increase water
and water reclamation rates, rate components, fees and service charges effective
February 6, 2026, and again to increase rates for four additional annual cycles on
January 1, 2027, January 1, 2028, January 1, 2029, January 1, 2030, as outlined in the
resolution and rate study included in the agenda materials.
Staff held two open house meetings regarding rate changes to inform the public about
the suggested rates, provide the basis for the suggested rate adjustments, and provide
an opportunity for the public to ask questions. One open house was held on November
13, 2025 at the Marana Municipal Complex and another was held on November 19,
2025 at Rattlesnake Ridge Elementary School. 
Because of the length of the comprehensive fee schedule revisions, a separate public
records resolution has been prepared and included as part of this item. 
If adopted, the proposed ordinance will implement the water and water reclamation
rate increases recommended by Raftelis.
Financial Impact:
In anticipation of the analysis, the current FY26 department budget did include
increases related to proposed rate changes effective after implementation date. As such,
pending approval by Council, an increase to the rates effective February 2026 will
increase revenues beyond budget expectations for the remainder of this fiscal year.
This item will allow the Water Department to increase revenue and meet the cost of
service needs identified in the report.
Staff Recommendation:
Staff recommends adoption of Ordinance No. 2026.001, amending the Town of Marana
comprehensive fee schedule by successively increasing the water base fee, water
consumption rates, groundwater resource/acquisition fee, monthly wastewater service
fee, and wastewater volume charge for all Marana residential and non-residential
water and wastewater customers effective February 6, 2026, January 1, 2027, January 1,
2028, January 1, 2029, and January 1, 2030; and adoption of Resolution No. 2026-004,
making the changes a public record.
 
Suggested Motion:
I move to adopt Ordinance No. 2026.001, amending the Town of Marana
comprehensive fee schedule by successively increasing the water base fee, water
consumption rates, groundwater resource/acquisition fee, monthly wastewater service
fee, and wastewater volume charge for all Marana residential and non-residential
water and wastewater customers effective February 6, 2026, January 1, 2027, January 1,
2028, January 1, 2029, and January 1, 2030; and to adopt Resolution No. 2026-004,
making the changes a public record.

Town of Marana | Water and Water Reclamation Rate Study 1 
 
1. EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
BACKGROUND 
The Town of Marana (Town) engaged Raftelis to conduct a water and water reclamation rate study. Raftelis 
worked closely with Town staff to develop an in-depth understanding of each utility’s finances and operations 
to develop the recommendations contained in this report . The rate study process invo lves a 5-year forecast of 
revenues and expenses and an evaluation of the sufficiency of current revenues to cover projected expenses. To 
the extent that a gap between current revenues and expenses is forecasted, rate adjustments are recommended 
to align revenues and expenses over the period. 
 
FINDINGS AND RECOMMENDATIONS 
For both water and water reclamation, revenues are not sufficient to cover projected expenses over the 5-year 
period. This gap is driven by increased operating expenses associated with maintaining a growing water and 
water reclamation system as well as addressing regulatory requirements. For water, capital expenses are also a 
significant driver, particularly those driven by addressing Per- and Polyfluoroalkyl Substances (PFAS) in the 
treatment of the Town’s groundwater.  Capital needs for water reclamation are more modest, with the largest 
upcoming project (the expansion of the Town’s Water Reclamation Facility) funded through impact fees, rather 
than rates.  
 
We recommend annual rate adjustments of 10.5% per year for water and 2.5% per year for water reclamation 
from FY 2026 to FY 2030. The first adjustment would be effective for bills rendered on, or after, February 6, 
2026, with subsequent adjustments effective each January 1 st. Based on these recommendations, Figure 1 
indicates the impact on the typical residential user  with both services, an increase of approximately 6 -7% per 
year.

Town of Marana | Water and Water Reclamation Rate Study 4 
 
2. INTRODUCTION 
2.1. Town of Marana Water and Water Reclamation 
System 
The Town began serving water customers in 1997 with approximately 500 customers.  Since that time, the 
Town’s system has grown to approximately 12,600 water customers and 7,900 water reclamation customers 
and 7 water systems.  The Town serves water customers through 14 groundwater wells, treated as needed to  
maintain drinking water quality and distributed to customers through a network of over 218 miles of distribution 
mains throughout the  Town’s service area. The Town collects and conveys customer wastewater through a 
network of 106 miles of sewer collection mains to a 1.5 million gallon per day (mgd) water reclamation facility 
where it is treated and disinfected for discharge into recharge basins, which the Town uses to generate 
groundwater recharge credits. 
 
The Town’s utility infrastructure is relatively young with an average age of 12 years for water infrastructure and 
14 years for water reclamation infrastructure. The Town’s system is growing, with a projected addition of 2,700 
equivalent dwelling units for water and 2,300 equivalent dwelling units for water reclamation over the next 5 
years alone.  Additional infrastructure required to serve new customers will be funded through the Town’s 
impact fees, mitigating the impact of growth related infrastructure on existing customers. In addition to 
addressing growth, the Town also faces increasing regulatory requirements related to Per- and Polyfluoroalkyl 
Substances (PFAS), requiring new and additional treatment processes to maintain compliance. The Town 
employs a dedicated staff of 45 across 7 divisions: Water Distribution, Production and Water Quality, Water 
Resources, Engineering, Business Services and Water Reclamation. 
Figure 4: Marana Water System 
 

Town of Marana | Water and Water Reclamation Rate Study 5 
 
2.2. Study Background 
2.2.1. Study Purpose 
The Town’s water and water reclamation utilities are enterprise funds, meaning they must operate like a 
business and charge rates to customers to cover the cost of providing them service. While the Town budgets for 
the cost of providing utility service annually, the budget is heavily influenced by the need to repair and replace 
utility infrastructure, the cost of which can fluctuate from year to year. To account for this the Town, like many 
communities through the United States, conducts periodic rate studies, which evaluate costs over multiple years 
to allow for planning and smoothing the impact of potential fluctuations on customer bills. 
 
The Town conducts rate studies approximately every 5 years to ensure that water and water reclamation rates 
produce sufficient revenue to support the delivery of safe and reliable utility service. The Town’s last study was 
completed in 2019 and following its completion, the Town adopted water and water reclamation rates for 2020 
through 2024, effective January 1 of each year. 
 
To conduct the rate study, Raftelis worked closely with staff to review operational, financial and customer data 
to develop a strategic financial plan to fund water and water reclamation operations over the next 5 fiscal years. 
The key outcome s of the plan are rate recommendations which fund the operating and capital expenditures 
necessary to provide service to customers in a financially sustainable manner. 
 
2.2.2. Staff Acknowledgements 
To conduct the study , Raftelis relied heavily on the institutional knowledge and experience of the Town’s 
internal experts. Successful completion of the study would not have been possible without significant 
collaboration with staff. Accordingly, we thank the following individuals for their assistance in providing the 
necessary data, reviewing the technical analysis and providing feedback on study deliverables: 
• Terry Rozema, Town Manager 
• Erik Montague, Deputy Town Manager 
• Yiannis Kalitzidis, Finance Director 
• Heidi Lasham, Director | Marana Water 
• Mike Osborne, Deputy Director | Marana Water 
• Sheila Nylund, Business Financial Manager 
• Emily Liscum, Business Services Supervisor 
• Paul Martinez, Water Operations Manager 
• Thomas Kibler, Water Reclamation Operations Manager 
• Jerry Huerstel, Water Resource Administrator  
• Francis Hemmah, Water Engineering Manager 
 
2.3. Report Organization 
The remainder of this report is organized into three sections.  
» Section 3 overviews the rate study process, including the fund structure the Town uses to account for 
water and water reclamation costs, the process of developing the multi-year financial plans and the key 
financial metrics which drive the financial forecast and rate recommendations.  
 
» Section 4 overviews the financial plans, which identify the level of revenue necessary to fund ongoing 
operations and capital repair and replacement in a financially sustainable manner.  
Town of Marana | Water and Water Reclamation Rate Study 6 
 
 
» Section 5 overviews our rate recommendations and customer impacts, based on the overall revenue 
needs identified in Section 4. 
Town of Marana | Water and Water Reclamation Rate Study 7 
 
3. RATE STUDY PROCESS 
3.1. Fund Structure 
The Town accounts for water and water reclamation utility costs in the following funds: 
 
Operating Funds. The water and water reclamation operating funds are the primary funds for the Town’s utilities. 
The primary source of revenue for these funds is rate revenue, generated from the Town’s monthly fixed charges and 
volume charges. Expenses paid from these funds include operating expenses and principal and interest payments 
(debt service) related to outstanding debt issued by the Town to fund capital improvements.  Each fund also makes 
transfers to the capital funds, as needed, to fund cash funded capital improvements. The operating funds, and the 
rates used to support them are the focus of the rate study.  
 
Capital Funds . The water and water reclamation capital funds are used to track funding for capital 
improvements needed for existing Town customers. The capital funds only account for repair and replacement 
projects related to existing customers. Growth related projects for new customers are accounted for in the 
Town’s impact fee funds. Sources of funding for the capital funds include proceeds from loans or bonds issued 
by the Town for debt financed capital improvements , transfers in from the operating funds  for cash financed 
capital improvements and grant funding provided from outside sources for applicable projects.  
 
The capital funds impact the rates indirectly, depending on how the projects are financed. Cash funded projects 
require transfers in from the operating fund s. Debt funded projects result in additional debt service, which is 
paid out of the operating funds. Grant funded projects, which are funded by outside sources, do not impact the 
operating funds or the rates. 
 
Impact Fee Funds . The impact fee funds operate like the capital funds, but track growth related capital 
improvements. The Town accounts for growth related capital improvements separately because they have a 
dedicated funding source: impact fees. Impact fees are one-time charges paid by developers to offset the cost of 
the additional infrastructure required to serve new customers, mitigating the impact on existing customers (i.e. 
“growth pays for growth”). Similar to projects for existing customers, growth related capital projects may be 
cash funded, or impact fee funded, but this funding does not impact rates . Some of the Town’s capital 
improvement projects benefit both existing and new customers. For those projects, the Town carefully allocates 
the cost of each project between the funds, such that existing customers (and rates) only cover the portion of 
the project that benefits them. 
 
The basis for the rate recommendations in the study is a financial plan, which identifies the level of revenue 
needed for the water and water reclamation operating funds to c over operating expenses and the cash flow 
impact of financing capital projects (transfers to the capital funds and debt service) 
 
3.2. Financial Planning Methodology  
3.2.1. Financial Planning Process 
The primary objective of financial planning involves comparing forecasted utility revenues under existing rates 
to forecasted expenditures and determining what annual adjustments to revenues are necessary to ensure the 
financial sustainability of the water and water reclamation utilities. The forecast for this study covers the Town’s 
Town of Marana | Water and Water Reclamation Rate Study 8 
 
Fiscal Year ending June 30, 2026 (FY 2026), through FY 2030. Given that the study is being completed shortly 
after the close of FY 2025, that year, its revenues, expenses and closing cash balances are also forecasted3. 
 
This involves the following three steps: 
 
Step 1: Forecast Baseline Revenue. Step 1 involves a forecast of customer accounts, usage and revenue in the 
absence of a rate increase. This revenue baseline is then compared to projected expenses (developed in Step 2), 
as part of a multi -year cash flow forecast (in Step 3) to determine whether existing revenues are sufficient to 
cover projected expenses. 
Step 2: Forecast Operating and Capital Expenses. Step 2 involves a forecast of operating expenses based on 
the Town’s budget, assumed inflation factors and any incremental costs associated with additional personnel 
or processes.  Capital expenses are forecasted based on the Town’s capital improvement projects and 
incorporated as annual cash outlays for capital projects or as principal and interest for debt financed projects. 
Step 3: Cash Flow Forecast and Revenue Sufficiency Evaluation . Step 3 involves a cash flow forecast of 
revenues (from Step 1) , and expenses (from Step 2) to assess the sufficiency of existing revenues to fund 
projected expenses and achieve appropriate financial metrics.  If revenues do not cover costs, a series of rate 
revenue adjustments will be recommended  over the multi -year period to bring revenues and expenses into 
alignment and to achieve the recommended financial performance targets. 
3.2.2. Financial Performance Targets 
The water and water reclamation utilities represent enterprise funds of the  Town, and—as a result—the rates 
and fees they charge need to be sufficient to cover the cost of providing service without support from any other 
Town funds. Assessing the sufficiency of water and water reclamation  rates involves assessing financial 
performance targets related to liquidity and debt service coverage.  Both factors are indicators of the financial 
health of a utility and the measurement of both is best practice for municipal utilities. 
 
Days Cash on Hand is a common measure of liquidity and the ability of the utility to deal with unanticipated 
declines in revenue or emergency expenditures without reducing service quality or dramatically increasing rates. 
Without cash already available, a utility is left with much less palatable options for addressing emergencies such 
as emergency rate increases, or temporary borrowing from other Town funds. Days cash on hand is expressed 
as: (available cash/expenses) x 365, with the resultant value being equivalent to the number of days expenses a 
utility could pay from available cash. A value of 30, for example, would indicate that a utility could cover 30 
days of expenses from existing cash.  
 
Our recommendation for the  Town is a minimum of 90 days of O&M. This balance is available to provide 
working capital, rate stabilization and emergency capital  funding. Working capital refers to the timing 
difference between when expenses are incurred and revenues received. A typical working capital allowance is 
1/8 of expenses. Rate stabilization refers to funds set aside to offset or smooth into a rate increase, reducing the 
immediate impact on customers. A typical target for rate stabilization is 5-10% of annual rate revenues. Finally, 
reserves for emergency capital expenditures can be used to cash fund or pre-fund (in the event bonds will later 
be issued) repairs or replacement to critical infrastructure . Targets for emergency capital are often tied to a 
hypothetical emergency repair (e.g., the estimated costs to repa ir a critical component of water transmission 
infrastructure). 
 
3 Staff provided estimated operating expenses and capital spending and carry-over for FY 25. Ending FY 25 cash 
balances by fund are based on ending balances on 6/30/2025, plus projected revenues and expenses for FY 25.  
Town of Marana | Water and Water Reclamation Rate Study 9 
 
To provide a concrete example, based on the cash flow forecast in Section 3.3, water expenses are projected to 
be $11 million in 2026, which suggests a minimum balance of approximately $ 2,700,000. This could be sub -
divided into the above categories as follows: 
» Working Capital: $1,375,000 ($11,000,000 x 1/8) 
» Rate Stabilization: $900,000 (10% of historical revenues of ~$9 million) 
» Emergency Capital: $425,000 remaining after working capital and rate stabilization 
 
We would not recommend that the Town formally establish separate funds for these categories, which can be 
administratively burdensome. Instead, they are more typically used as a tool for communicating the intent and 
level of cash held in reserve by policy. 
 
A debt service coverage ratio is the second metric used to evaluate utility financial performance. A debt service 
coverage ratio is calculated as: (revenues – operating expenses) / debt service. A ratio of 1 indicates that a utility 
is generating enough revenue to operate and repay its debt obligations with no room for unforeseen 
circumstances.  
 
A debt service coverage ratio of greater than one indicates that the utility can cover operating costs and repay 
debt with some room to spare. A debt service coverage ratio of greater than 1 also means that funds above and 
beyond operating expenses and debt service are being generated. These are typically used for cash funding of 
capital projects. A debt service coverage ratio of less than 1 indicates ongoing revenues are not sufficient to pay 
for operations and repay debt obligations and that cash on hand is being used to supplement current revenues. 
 
As described in further detail below, the Town’s capital improvement needs are significant , which will likely 
require additional borrowing over the next 5 years.  While the rates proposed in this study are based on cash 
flows (aligning revenues and expenses) over the 5 years, we recommend the Town work towa rd at least 1.0 
coverage for the debt covered by utility rates , assuming the Town can employ sources of financing without a 
specific coverage requirement. If the Town issues additional bonds with a specific debt service coverage ratio, 
a higher target may be appropriate.  
   
Town of Marana | Water and Water Reclamation Rate Study 10 
 
4. Water and Water Reclamation 
Financial Plans 
The principal objective of the water and water reclamation financial plans is to determine the level of revenue 
required to fund projected operating and capital expenses  while maintaining alignment with financial 
performance targets for days cash on hand and debt service coverage. The development of the water and water 
reclamation financial plans for the Town involves a forecast of baseline revenues (Step 1); a forecast of operating 
and capital expenses (Step 2) and a cash flow forecast and revenue sufficiency evaluation (Step 3). 
 
4.1. Step 1 – Forecast Baseline Revenue 
Determining revenue under the  Town’s existing rate levels is the first step in developing the financial plan s. 
These revenues form the baseline (i.e. in the absence of any action to adjust rates) against which projected 
expenditures are compared. Step 1 answers the question: “what would our revenues be, if we did not take any 
action to adjust rates?” Baseline revenue is a function of customer accounts and usage over the forecast period.  
Account growth is impacted by development in the  Town, which is itself a function of a wide range of 
macroeconomic factors , Town policies targeted at promoting development , and regional competition for 
development.  
 
One point of reference for account growth are the Town’s Infrastructure Improvement Plan’s (IIPs), which are 
planning documents that identify the capital improvements required associated with growth over a 10 year 
period. These plans are informed by land use assumptions documents which identify the amount and types of 
development which is anticipated to occur over a 10 year period. These planning documents are used to develop 
impact fees which are used to offset growth related capital improvements as described above. The Town’s 2022 
IIPs projected equivalent dwelling unit (EDU) growth of approximately 1,200 EDUs per year for water and 
900 EDUs per year for water reclamation.  
 
That said, since these documents are used for infrastructure planning, they are somewhat conservative in that 
they err on the side of higher growth assumptions to ensure the recommended  infrastructure improvements are 
not undersized. Accordingly, for conservatism and to use the latest available data,  Raftelis employed EDU 
projections provided by the Town’s Development Services staff  as the basis for account growth forecasts over 
the next 5 years.  Based on these forecasts account growth is anticipated t o be 780 EDUs for water and 660 
EDUs for reclamation this fiscal year (FY 26), with an average of 480 EDUs and 400 EDUs for water and water 
reclamation, respectively for FY 2027-FY 2030. 
 
Usage is impacted by both short and long -run factors. In the short -run (year to year) usage is impacted by 
weather, with higher temperatures and lower precipitation driving increased usage and vice versa. In the long 
run, utilities throughout the United States are seeing declining per capita consumption due to increased fixture 
and appliance efficiency and an increased conservation ethos among customers.  To account for anticipated 
conservation, we have assumed a slightly lower usage growth rate (1% less) than the anticipated growth in 
customer accounts, to factor in anticipated conservation. 
 
Baseline revenue is calculated by multiplying the forecast number of bills (accounts x 12) by the fixed charges 
and forecast usage by the volume charges. Table 1 and Table 2 indicate the baseline revenue for water and water 
Town of Marana | Water and Water Reclamation Rate Study 11 
 
reclamation respectively. Also shown are non-rate revenues, which are derived from fees the Town charges for 
specific services charged to the recipient of the service at the time the service is rendered.  These non -rate 
revenues offset the cost of these services to specific customers, which would otherwise be incorporated into the 
rates of all customers. The projection of non -rate revenue is based on the Town’s budget for FY 2026 , and 
assumed to be flat for FY 2027 to FY 20304. 
 
Impact fee revenues are not included in the revenues shown below because they are used solely to finance 
impact fee projects. As described above, the focus of a rate study is to determine rates to cover costs incurred to 
serve existing customers. Growth related infrastructure costs and impact fee revenue are accounted for in 
separate impact fee funds (as described in Section 3.1) and do not impact rates for existing customers.

4.2. Step 2 – Forecast Operating Expenses 
Operating expenses are those which the utility incurs on a consistent day to day basis, and which do not involve 
the construction of a capital asset. The basis for the operating expense forecast was the Town’s FY 2026 budget. 
From this we removed any capital expenses which we forecast separately. For projections beyond the budget 
year, there are 3 factors which impact the forecast: 
 
Budget performance. To the extent that the utility tends to outperform in certain areas (i.e., spend less than 
budgeted), realization adjustments are incorporated so that the projection of baseline O&M (i.e., before any 
incremental expenses and inflationary adjustments) is a reasonable reflection of what is likely to occur.  Based 
on discussions with  Town staff, there is not a material difference between budgeted and actual expenses. 
Accordingly, no adjustment was made. 
 
Inflation. The Town’s utilities purchase goods and services like any other consumer and are therefore subject 
to the same inflationary pressures.  Figure 5 below indicates the year over year change in the consumer price 
index (CPI), excluding food and energy, often referred to as “Core Inflation”. As shown, the years immediately 
following the COVID-19 pandemic were marked by significantly higher inflation. The Town’s previous study 
was conducted pre-COVID before the r ecent round of high inflation.  While the previous rate study assumed 
inflationary increases, it did not incorporate the unprecedented inflation that has occurred over the past few 
years. While the rate of change has decreased, the prices themselves remain at elevated levels. Further, utilities 
also face inflation pressures from tariffs  and potential interest rate adjustments by the United States F ederal 
Reserve5.  
 
 
5 Tariffs impact any imported products that serve as utility inputs but can also impact domestically produced products 
that are partially produced overseas. The primary impact of Federal Reserve action would be interest rate reductions, 
which—depending on the size and pace of the adjustments—could push general inflation higher.

Incremental expenses. As the Town continues to grow it will incur incremental operating expenses associated 
with serving a larger customer base and maintaining additional infrastructure. Since inflation alone is not 
sufficient to capture these incremental costs , Town staff reviewed the applicable budget items to determine 
appropriate forecasts for the relevant expenses . The major categories of incremental costs include additional 
staff and related vehicles, construction equipment, water quality test expenses and contracted services. 
 
• Staffing. In total 13 additional positions were included in the projections: 6 for water, 4 for water 
reclamation, 1 shared position and 2 interns. For water staff, the Town employed benchmarking data 
from the American Water Works Association to guide growth in staffing levels as the Town’s water 
system grows. For water reclamation, staffing levels were based on the Town’s 2016 Water Reclamation 
Facility Master Plan . The cost of purchasing vehicles for the additional staff , and additional 
maintenance on those vehicles was also included. 
 
• Construction Equipment. These anticipated purchases include a backhoe, tractor, skidster, excavator, 
forklift, vacuum truck, camera van and telehandler. These one-time purchases were incorporated into 
the forecast in the expected year of purchase based on the anticipated cost.  
 
• Water Quality Expenses. As the Town adds additional treatment facilities, additional water quality 
testing will be required to meet regulatory requirements.

4.3. Step 2 – Forecast Capital Expenses 
Capital expenses are incurred to finance the construction of capital assets used to deliver water and water 
reclamation service to customers. There are two primary sources of financing for capital projects: cash, which 
represents funding out of current resources (today’s revenues, today’s available cash), and referred to as pay-as-
you-go or PAYGO capital ; and debt, which represents funding available today, but repaid over time, with 
interest.  
 
The Town has historically funded major capital improvements using debt financing. This strategy is common 
among municipal utilities because the improvements being financed have long useful lives (40-50+ years). The 
use of debt spreads the cost of the asset over its useful life and, in so doing, also spreads the impact over all the 
potential users of a project (today and 10-20 years down the road). 
 
Forecasted capital expenditures to be recovered from rates include principal and interest payments on 
previously debt financed projects, and funding for new projects either from cash, or additional debt financing  
based on a capital financing plan. 
 
4.3.1.1. Existing Debt 
In order to determine the impact on rates, the portion of utility debt associated with projects for existing 
customers must be identified.  Debt service for growth related projects (or portions of projects) is tracked

4.3.1.2. Capital Financing Plan 
To determine the capital expenses associated with new projects, Raftelis worked with staff to develop a capital 
financing plan for major water and water reclamation capital improvement projects anticipated over the next 5 
years. For each project, Raftelis and staff identified the relevant funds (i.e., water operations, water 
infrastructure impact etc.) and anticipated funding sources. Funding sources include cash, proceeds from new 
debt and grant proceeds. 
 
Cash financed projects are funded by cash already available in the capital  funds and additional transfers from 
the operating funds. Grants include American Re scue Plan Act (ARPA) funds and WIFA grants.  For debt 
financed projects  the Town will continue to pursue additional grant opportunities as well as low interest 
financing from WIFA. Since these sources are competitive and not guaranteed, a more conservative debt 
financing assumption (20 years, 5%) was incorporated for debt financed projects. 
 
For water, the biggest projects are related to addressing Per- and Polyfluoroalkyl Substances (PFAS) through 
additional treatment processes at its well sites. For reclamation, the largest capital improvement project is the 
Phase II expansion of the Water Reclamation Facility (WRF) and recharge basins. The vast majority of this 
project will be funded through impact fees, with a small portion —related to an administrative building at the 
WRF site, included in rates.

5. Rate Recommendations 
5.1. Recommendation 
Raftelis recommends increasing all water rates by 10.5% per year over the next 5 years and increasing all water 
reclamation rates by 2.5% per year over the next 5 years. Table 10 indicates the recommended rates for FY 2026 
to FY 2030. The FY 2026 rates would be effective for bills rendered, on or after February 6, 2026. Subsequent 
adjustments would be applied to bills rendered on, or after, January 1st of each year. The winter implementation 
will mitigate the impact of the adjustments  by implementing the change when usage and bills are typically 
lower.

5.2. Customer Impacts 
The impact on a typical  residential customer with a 5/8” meter with 7,000 gallons of water usage and 6,000 
gallons of water reclamation usage  is shown in Figure 6 below. As indicated, the impact on a user with both 
services would be around 6-7% per year.

based on rates currently in effect as of September 2025. Differences in rates are driven by a variety of factors 
including density, economies of scale and the level and timing of capital improvements by community.

Figure 8 indicates a comparison of the combined bill at different household sizes8 to median household income. 
While there is no 100% objective measure of affordability, 4.5% of median household income is a commonly 
used threshold. As shown below the proposed 2026 bill is well under 4.5% of median household income for all 
household sizes which suggests that the proposed increases will not pose significant affordability challenges for 
most customers9.
