A. Background  
Data centers are specialized facilities 
designed to house computer systems, servers 
and related hardware that store, process and 
manage vast amounts of digital information.  
Data centers have become the backbone of 
U.S. technological advancement, driving 
economic growth and securing national 
leadership in innovation.  Through cloud 
computing, they support online education, 
empower communities to advance seamless 
connectivity, and host artiﬁcial intelligence 
(“AI”) systems that drive personalized 
experiences, real-time navigation and critical 
decision-making tools.  Demand for data 
centers is supported by a diverse set of 
industries with durable long-term 
employment and positive ﬁscal impacts for 
local communities.   
Long-term infrastructure investment 
supports communities within Pima County 
(the “County”), and speciﬁcally the Town of 
Marana (the “Town”), playing a leading role 
in an increasingly dynamic and 
interconnected 21st century economy.  
Approximately 310 acres (Parcel No. 208-09-
001E, the “Property”) located west of 
Interstate 10 (“I-10”) along N. Luckett Road 
has been identiﬁed as an ideal site for this 
type of investment.  (See Exhibit I.A: Regional 
Context.) 
 
Exhibit I.A : Regional Context
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006
SECTION I: INTRODUCTION & POLICY           LUCKETT ROAD SOUTH  SPECIFIC PLAN 
 
 
 
 
3 
 
B. Project Overview  
Fremont Peak Properties, LLC (the “Applicant”) 
is interested in developing the Property with a 
data center and related infrastructure 
improvements (the “Project”).  The Project, 
entitled through the Luckett Road South 
Speciﬁc Plan (“Speciﬁc Plan”), positions the 
Town to become a hub for technology, 
innovation and business growth.  (See Exhibit 
I.B.1: Speciﬁc Plan Boundaries.) 
The Project will create high-quality jobs 
related to the Information Technology sector, 
which has been targeted as one of four key 
industries for employment growth within the 
Town.   
This Speciﬁc Plan has been drafted in 
conformance with Marana Town Code 
(“MTC”) § 17-6-13, Data Centers.  Potential 
impacts to adjacent uses will be minimized 
through strategic mitigation measures, as 
outlined in Section IV, Development & Design 
Regulations, such as: 
• limiting building heights to diminish 
visual impacts; 
• incorporating adequate drainage 
infrastructure to oﬀset an increase in 
impervious surfaces; and 
• providing ample setbacks and 
buﬀeryards for sound attenuation.  
 
 
Exhibit I.B .1: Specific Plan Boundaries
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006
SECTION I: INTRODUCTION & POLICY           LUCKETT ROAD SOUTH  SPECIFIC PLAN 
 
 
 
 
4 
 
 
 
Exhibit I. B.2 : General Plan Land Use  
C. General Plan Amendment 
The Property is located in the General Plan’s North 
Growth Area, which is “envisioned to become a premier 
employment hub in Marana, concentrated around Pinal 
Airpark [that] will attract manufacturing, distribution, 
and logistics industries for its advantageous location 
between Pinal Airpark, Interstate 10, and the Union 
Paciﬁc Railroad, as well as the future Interstate 11 
corridor.”  As shown on Exhibit I.B.2, the General Plan 
Future Land Use Map designates the Property as 
Employment, which supports a range of industrial, 
warehousing, manufacturing, business parks and other 
uses that present employment opportunities.  The 
Project will result in signiﬁcant infrastructure 
investment in North Marana, not only funding this 
Project, but also potentially serving as a catalyst for 
additional economic development in this area.   
Concurrent with this Speciﬁc Plan proposal is a request 
to amend the Property’s land use designation from 
Employment to Master Planned Area (“MPA”).  Make 
Marana 2040’s Table 1-1 indicates a change from 
Employment to MPA qualiﬁes as a minor amendment.  
The MPA designation “denotes areas that are guided by 
separate development approvals.  These approvals 
establish the land uses, densities and intensities of 
development, and the character of the area.”  MPA is 
meant for properties appropriately planned and 
designed within the context of its environment.  This 
Speciﬁc Plan is consistent with the intent of the MPA 
designation and the vision for the Town’s North Growth 
Area as a future employment center, as well as the 
goals and policies of Make Marana 2040.

A. Existing Land Use  & Zoning

As depicted in Exhibit II.A: Existing Land

and is currently used for agricultural 
purposes.  Make Marana 2040 designates 
this Property as Employment on the Future 
Land Use Map.  (Refer to Exhibit I.B.2: 
General Plan Land Use in Section I.) 
 
2. Surrounding Properties  
Exhibit II.A identiﬁes the land uses and 
zoning of surrounding properties.  
Adjacent properties in all directions are 
currently farmed and have no existing 
onsite structures.  Properties to the 
northwest and southwest are currently 
used for solar energy production and 
battery storage.  The Arizona Veteran’s 
Memorial Cemetery is located northeast of 
the Property and includes three single-
story structures.  
All adjacent properties are also designated 
Employment by Make Marana 2040.

B. Topography 
1. Pre -Development Cross -Slope  
The majority of the Property is relatively 
ﬂat with gradual sloping from east to west 
and approximately 16 to 18 feet of fall 
across the Property.  As shown on Exhibit 
II.B, the west portion of the Property is 
generally 12 to 14 feet lower in elevation 
than the east portion.  The Property does 
not contain areas with slopes 15% or 
greater. 
 
2. Significant Topographic Features  
There are no hillside conservation areas, 
rock outcrops, or signiﬁcant topographic 
features on the Property.  Areas around 
the northwest and southwest corners of 
the Property are graded to serve existing 
agricultural purposes.

C. Hydrology  
The Property and surrounding area are subject to runoﬀ originating in the 
Tortolita Mountains to the east that is conveyed via bridges and culverts 
through the Central Arizona Project (“CAP”) Canal and the Interstate 10/Union 
Paciﬁc Railroad (“UPRR”) transportation corridor .  West of Interstate 10, runoﬀ 
ﬂows overland primarily in a sheet ﬂow manner to the north and west toward 
the Santa Cruz River.  Runoﬀ impacting the Property is deﬁned by the Tortolita 
Alluvial Fan Study (“TAFS”), which is the basis for eﬀective Federal Emergency 
Management Administration (“FEMA”) Flood Zone designations in this area.  
The total drainage area within the TAFS models (HEC-1 and FLO-2D) is 174.9 
square miles.  Exhibit II.C.1 depicts the Property within the TAFS study area.  
Signiﬁcant alterations have been made to the terrain in the vicinity due to 
historic agricultural activity.  A higher resolution HEC-RAS 2D model was 
created to better model ﬂow patterns in the Project vicinity.  The RAS 2D 
model utilizes inﬂow hydrographs generated by the eﬀective TAFS model.   
Runoﬀ impacts the Property as sheet ﬂow across farm ﬁelds separated by 
graded roads and irrigation canals.  Exhibit II.C.2 depicts the drainage patterns 
across the Property and the 100-year peak discharges at key cross sections.  
The Property is characterized by shallow sheet ﬂooding.  The 100-year sheet 
ﬂood depths and peak discharges are depicted on Exhibit II.C.2.   
Most of the Property is inundated by the 100-year ﬂood.  The majority of the 
Property lies within FEMA Zone AO1, with a large area of Zone X (shaded) to 
the east and a small area in the southwest corner.  (See Exhibit II.C.2.)  Runoﬀ 
exits the Property to the north and west in a sheet ﬂow manner, with depths 
typically ranging between 1 and 2 feet.    
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006

D. Vegetation & Wildlife  
The majority of the Property is currently used 
for agriculture.  The Property contains no 
signiﬁcant groups of vegetation or riparian 
habitat.  There are small, vegetated areas near

Property, as shown on Exhibit II.D.  The

E. Soils & Geology 
The primary use of the Property is agricultural with irrigation channels.  A 
preliminary geotechnical engineering ﬁeld exploration was conducted, and 
preliminary borings generally encountered soft to medium, stiﬀ, ﬁne-grained 
soils and loose sand soils with variable amounts of silt, clay and gravel to 
depths of approximately 13 to 30 feet below the existing ground surface.  
These upper soils were generally overlying very stiﬀ to hard clay soils and 
dense to very dense sand soils.  Groundwater was not encountered in any test 
boring at the time of drilling.

2. Water  
As shown on Exhibit II.J.2, the Property is 
located within the Marana Water Intended 
Service Area and adjacent to the existing 
Cortaro-Marana Irrigation District (“CMID”) 
canal.  The CMID currently provides water to

A. Land Use  
The Property has been identiﬁed as an ideal site for future data center development.  
The Applicant intends to develop the Property with a data center and related 
infrastructure improvements that are already in high demand in northern Marana.   
Data centers are specialized facilities designed to house computer systems, chips, 
servers and other related hardware that store, process and manage vast amounts of 
digital information.  Data centers are the backbone of U.S. technological advancement, 
driving economic growth and securing national leadership in innovation.  Through 
cloud computing, they support online education and video conferencing, empower 
communities to advance seamless digital connectivity, and host systems that generate 
personalized experiences, real-time navigation and critical decision-making tools.  
Demand for data centers is supported by a diverse set of industries with durable long-
term employment and positive ﬁscal impacts for local communities.   
The Speciﬁc Plan positions the Town to become a hub for technology, innovation and 
business growth, and provides the Town with a unique opportunity to play a critical 
role in an increasingly dynamic and interconnected 21st century economy.  This 
investment will also create high-quality jobs in the Information Technology sector, 
which has been targeted as one of four key industries for employment growth within 
the Town.   
This Speciﬁc Plan has been drafted in conformance with MTC § 17-6-13, Data Centers.  
Potential impacts to adjacent uses will be minimized through strategic mitigation 
measures, as outlined in Section IV, Development & Design Regulations, such as: 
• limiting building heights to diminish visual impacts; 
• incorporating adequate drainage infrastructure to oﬀset an increase 
in impervious surfaces; and 
• providing ample setbacks and buﬀeryards for sound attenuation. 
The Property is located in the General Plan’s North Growth Area, which is “envisioned 
to become a premier employment hub in Marana, concentrated around Pinal Airpark 
[that] will attract manufacturing, distribution, and logistics industries for its 
advantageous location between Pinal Airpark, Interstate 10, and the Union Paciﬁc 
Railroad, as well as the future Interstate 11 corridor.”  The General Plan Future Land 
Use Map designates the Property as Employment, which supports a range of 
industrial, warehousing, manufacturing, business parks and other uses that present 
employment opportunities.  The Project will result in signiﬁcant infrastructure 
investment in North Marana, not only funding this Project, but also potentially serving 
as a catalyst for additional economic development in this area.   
A Conceptual Land Use Plan is provided as Exhibit III.A to illustrate how the Project 
may be developed.

B. Topography & Grading  
The proposed grading design will be largely inﬂuenced by oﬀsite drainage 
conveyance through and around the Property, as well as the minimum 
required ﬁnished ﬂoor elevation building pads.  The building Finished Floor 
Elevations (“FFE”) will be constructed in accordance with FEMA and Town 
requirements as follows: in proposed CLOMR/LOMR ﬂood zone mapping Zone 
Shaded X, A or AE, a minimum of 1 foot above the 100-year WSEL; Zone AO-1, 
2 feet above highest adjacent natural grade (“HANG”); Zone AO-2, 3 feet 
above HANG; AO-3, 4 feet above HANG.  Areas not impacted by a mapped 
FEMA Zone (Unshaded Zone X) will have no minimum FFE requirement or a 
minimum of 2 feet above the highest adjacent local 100-year WSEL.  The ﬁll 
pads are proposed to be generally sloping at a 1% grade to the internal 
drainage basins.  
C. Traffic 
1. General Onsite Circulation  
The Project proposes two access driveways along Luckett Road on the east side of 
the Property.  Each access drive is assumed to have one ingress lane and one 
egress lane with full access to Luckett Road.  Internal access lanes will be 
constructed per Town standards. 
Regional access to the Property will be via Pinal Airpark Road from the north by 
extending Luckett Road into Pinal County.  Exhibit III.C illustrates two options for 
the Luckett Road extension.

• A northbound left turn lane is warranted and recommended on 
Luckett Road at the South Driveway (Intersection 3).

accommodated by the existing movement storage.  Recommended auxiliary lanes 
shall have 150 feet of storage based on the minimum requirements except the 
westbound left turn lane on Pinal Airpark, which is recommended to have 475 feet 
of storage.

D. Hydrology  
Exhibit III.D.1 depicts the drainage system under post-development conditions.  (Note: 
Exhibits III.D.1 and III.D.2 depict two diﬀerent drainage development approaches, as 
discussed below.) 
The Conceptual Land Use Plan (Exhibit III.A) includes light industrial buildings and 
utility yards with interconnecting road access.  The development areas will be elevated 
above the 100-year ﬂood.  Oﬀsite runoﬀ will be captured upstream of the ﬁll areas in 
below-grade earthen perimeter channels.  The oﬀsite ﬂows will be conveyed around 
the elevated portions of the Property into multiple ﬂow release basins located along 
the north and west Property lines.  The constructed channels conveying higher ﬂow 
concentrations are designed to ﬂow at low velocities but may require scour protection 
at speciﬁc transition points to alleviate erosive potential.   
The perimeter drainage concept is designed to release runoﬀ as shallow sheet ﬂow at 
the same locations as existing conditions, consistent with the Town of Marana 
Drainage Manual (“Drainage Manual”).  The downstream basins are sized to release 
runoﬀ to the north and west over low-level containment weirs without increasing 
downstream 100-year water surface elevations more than 0.1 feet relative to existing 
conditions.  The intent is to have no impact with regards to ﬂow depths or erosion 
hazards to the existing downstream ﬂoodplain areas.  This “no rise” design approach is 
consistent with the Town’s development requirements and will serve as the basis for 
eventual Federal Emergency Management Agency (“FEMA”) Conditional Letter of Map 
Revision (“CLOMR”) and Letter of Map Revision (“LOMR”) applications to remove the 
ﬂood zone designations within the Property.  FEMA CLOMR/LOMR map revision 
applications will be based on eﬀective TAFS FLO-2D hydrology, based on non-regional 
rainfall, in accordance with Section 4.1 of the Drainage Manual.  It is anticipated that 
the map revision applications will utilize two dimensional HEC-RAS software for 
updated hydraulic modeling of the post-development ﬂoodplain, in accordance with 
the Drainage Manual. 
Onsite runoﬀ increases due to substantially increased impervious areas will be 
mitigated in accordance with the Drainage Manual with the installation of multiple 
interior retention basins, which will be sized to capture the total onsite ﬂow volume 
from successive 100-year and 10-year rainfall events.  All impervious development 
areas will drain into the interior basins.  Each basin will include low-ﬂow outlets to 
allow the basins to gradually drain into the perimeter drainage channels.  The 
downstream basins, and possibly the interior basins, will require drywells to allow the 
basins to fully drain within the maximum storage time allowed. 
As the upstream ADMP alternative channels have not yet been constructed, one 
option would be for construction of an oversized version of the CMID 10.5 channel 
within new public ROW along the north Property line and extending downstream the 
full distance to the Santa Cruz River outfall.  Design approval and construction of the 
CMID 10.5 channel will require CLOMR and LOMR applications to the Town and FEMA.  
The oversized channel would allow capture of substantially more runoﬀ, thereby 
reducing the requirements for onsite ﬁll and drainage infrastructure.  The Applicant 
will work with the Town to design the CMID 10.5 channel as a water recharge 
structure, if feasible.  Under this option, the onsite retention requirements would be 
accomplished via direct discharge to the Santa Cruz River, while onsite detention 
would be accomplished within the perimeter basins.  Exhibit III.D.2 depicts a 
conceptual drainage plan for this optional scenario.  The internal development areas 
would not require substantial ﬁll.

E. Vegetation & Wildlife  
The proposed Luckett Road extension will impact the Xeroriparian A riparian habitat 
located at the northeast corner of the Property.  The riparian vegetation will be 
mitigated through transplanting or replacing with plantings chosen to match adjacent 
undisturbed natural areas.  Additionally, located primarily in the riparian area, species 
of native plants, as listed on the Marana Protected Native Plant List, will be impacted 
by the Luckett Road extension.  The most notable of these species is the Prosopis, 
Parkinsonia and Acacia.  The native plant preservation plan will detail the mitigation 
approach for impacted vegetation.  The balance of the site, used for the proposed 
data center, is currently agricultural and does not contain riparian areas or protected 
native plant species.  
The yellow-billed cuckoo, western burrowing owl and southwestern willow ﬂycatcher 
are identiﬁed within 3 miles of, and may inhabit, the Property.  The developer and 
contractor have additional responsibilities prior to and during construction to mitigate 
the inﬂuence on these species: 
• The developer will employ a biologist to complete a pre-construction survey for 
burrowing owls prior to construction in all suitable habitats that will be 
disturbed.  The biologist shall possess a burrowing owl survey protocol training 
certiﬁcate issued by the Arizona Game and Fish Department. 
• Prior to construction, all personnel who will be onsite, including, but not limited 
to, contractors, contractors’ employees, supervisors, inspectors, and 
subcontractors shall review the “Western Burrowing Owl Awareness” ﬂyer .  
• If any burrowing owls or active burrows are identiﬁed, the contractor shall 
notify the developer immediately.  No construction activities shall take place 
within 100 feet of any active burrow.  If the developer, in cooperation with the 
biologist, determines that burrowing owls cannot be avoided, the contractor 
shall employ a qualiﬁed biologist holding a permit from the U.S. Fish and 
Wildlife Service to relocate burrowing owls from the Property, as appropriate. 
• If vegetation clearing occurs during the migratory bird breeding season (March 
1 – August 31), the contractor shall avoid any active bird nests.  If the active 
nests cannot be avoided, the contractor shall notify the engineer for evaluation.  
During the non-breeding season (September 1 – February 28), vegetation 
removal is not subject to this restriction. 
Based on the completed Biological Evaluation (provided as Appendix B), no potential 
for “Take” exists (meaning that the Project has no potential to harass, harm, pursue, 
hunt, shoot, wound, kill, trap, capture, or collect, or to attempt to engage in any such 
conduct) to threatened and endangered species. 
Potential Waters of the United States (WOTUS) are not located on the Property.  
Therefore, Section 404/401 permitting is not required or proposed for this 
development. 
 
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006

F. Utilities  
As described in Section II (Development Capability Report), public utilities are 
readily accessible for extension through the Property.  Services will be 
extended with proper design capacity to meet the demands of the Project.   
1. Sewer  
The Project will produce sanitary wastewater from bathrooms and other 
facilities, as well as a small amount of industrial wastewater on an 
infrequent basis.  These wastewater streams will be blended together  
The Project will discharge wastewater through a new 2-mile force main 
proposed to align with the Luckett Road public ROW from the Property, 
southerly to a point of connection to the existing 27-inch gravity sewer at 
the intersection with Sagebrush.  A new sewer lift station is required to 
transfer the domestic wastewater uphill to the existing line.  Final location 
of sewer lift station will be conﬁrmed through the design process. 
See Exhibit III.F.1.a: Sewer Intent to Serve Letter and Exhibit III.F.1.b: 
Proposed Wastewater.

2. Water  
The Project will draw water from two sources.   
For domestic use only, potable water will be provided by a pipeline 
extension and upsizing a portion of existing infrastructure.  The Project will 
connect to the Marana Water system via a 2.25-mile, 8-inch extension 
along Luckett Road, Hardin Road and Wentz Road to the intersection of 
Sagebrush Road.  The ﬁnal alignment will be determined by Town water 
modeling.  (See Exhibit III.F.2.a: Water Intent to Serve Letter and Exhibit 
III.F.2.b: Proposed Potable Water.) 
Industrial water and ﬁre water supply will be withdrawn from the adjacent 
CMID irrigation canal.  CMID has indicated an average supply rate of 12 
cubic feet per second, or 7.8 million gallons (“MG”) per day, is available.  
CMID has conﬁrmed capacity is available to supply the non-potable water 
to the site for industrial purposes.  The Project’s estimated operational 
non-potable water needs average approximately 18 AFY , with a ﬁre storage 
requirement of up to 2 AFY.  The Applicant will also work with CMID on the 
landscaping and construction wate r.  The current agricultural uses have an 
estimated demand of 800 to 1,100 AFY per year .   
The Project, at full build out, has an estimated average annual demand for 
both potable and non-potable water use of 40 AFY for all potential uses.   
A water storage tank and booster pump are required to meet ﬁre ﬂow 
requirements for the Project.   
A letter from CMID aﬃrming suﬃcient water is available to serve the 
Project is provided as Appendix C.

3. Electric Service  
The Project will utilize an estimated 550 to 750 megawatts (“MW”) of 
power at full buildout.  The Project’s consumption will ramp up over time, 
typically starting with approximately 10-20% initial load and increasing 
gradually to the full build capacity over the course of 2-3 years.  While 
these estimates are subject to change as the Project develops, the Project's 
power utilization will be subject to ﬁnalization through ongoing utility 
studies and subsequent energy supply agreements directly with the energy 
provider(s).  All energy supply agreements are subject to regulatory 
approval by the Arizona Corporation Commission (ACC). 
A letter from TEP aﬃrming engagement with the Applicant to serve power 
needs of the Project is provided as Appendix D. 
G. Public Services 
The Property is not currently located within a ﬁre district.  Annexation into 
Northwest Fire District will be required prior to issuance of building permits.  
As discussed in Subsection III.F.2 above, water for ﬁre water supply will be 
drawn from the adjacent CMID canal, and a water storage tank with booster 
station will be installed as part of this Project. 
There will be no impacts to schools or parks as a result of this Project. 
 
H. Recreation & Trails 
For security reasons, the interior of the Project will not be connected to a 
pedestrian/trail system outside of the secure perimeter.  Private amenities 
may be provided for employees within the interior of the Project. 
 
I. Cultural Resources 
A Class I Cultural Resources Report for the Property to be submitted under 
separate cover .

A. Applicability  
The standards and guidelines contained with this Speciﬁc Plan shall supersede 
the standards within the MTC.  If an issue, situation or condition arises that is 
not addressed by this Speciﬁc Plan, the Speciﬁc Plan shall be administered and 
interpreted per Section V.A of this Speciﬁc Plan. 
All construction and development within the Speciﬁc Plan area shall comply 
with applicable provisions of various codes and regulations adopted by the 
Town including, but not limited to, drainage, public water/wastewater 
infrastructure, building, ﬁre and lighting codes current at the time of 
development. 
 
B. Land Uses  
1. Primary  Uses  
• Data Center 
 
2. Ancillary Uses  
• Land uses ancillary to the Data Center use or in support of employees of 
the Data Center use, including: 
o Retail (food, beverage, oﬃce supplies, pharmacy) 
o Coﬀee shop/bakery/café/restaurant  
o Mail/shipping service 
o Recreation/gym/ﬁtness studio facilities 
o Buildings and grounds maintenance (landscaping, facilities or 
janitorial) 
o Recycling collection  
o Utility switchyards and substations with or without battery 
storage 
 Battery storage use is limited to the area designated for 
the switchyard/substation use 
o Ancillary renewable energy systems (including solar, wind or 
waste-to-energy) 
o Water treatment/auxiliary water building/water storage 
o Utility operations building 
o Central logistics & security buildings 
o Parks, trails and functional open space areas 
• Uses that are not expressly permitted but are similar in type, scale and 
intensity to other ancillary uses allowed within the Speciﬁc Plan, and 
which are not otherwise unlawful, injurious to the general health or 
welfare, or speciﬁcally excluded, may be approved by the Zoning 
Administrator or their authorized designee. 
 
3. Prohibited Uses  
• Besides the Data Center use, no other Primary Uses are permitted within 
this Speciﬁc Plan, including, but not limited to, Mineral Extraction.

D. Design Standards  
The following design standards apply to the Project. 
1. Noise Attenuation  
a. Baseline Sound Study 
An Acoustic Report was prepared to determine baseline (ambient) noise 
levels surrounding the Property.  (See Appendix E.)   
Noise contour exhibits depicting the anticipated noise levels to be 
generated by the data center are included in the Acoustic Modeling 
Results, provided as Appendix F. 
Except for construction work, the maximum sound levels for Project 
operations adjacent to speciﬁc land uses will be as follows: 
ADJACENT USE MAXIMUM dBA DAYTIME/NIGHTTIME* 
RESIDENTIAL/MIXED-USE 55/55 
AGRICULTURE 60/55 
COMMERCIAL 65/60 
INDUSTRIAL 72/65 
*“Daytime” is deﬁned as the hours between 7:00AM to 7:00PM on weekdays, or between 9:00AM to 
8:00PM on Saturdays, Sundays, and legal holidays observed by the Town.  “Nighttime” is deﬁned in the 
code as between 7:00PM to 7:00AM on weekdays, or between 8:00PM to 9:00AM on Saturdays, 
Sundays, and legal holidays observed by the Town. 
 
 
The Project will incorporate sound mitigation methods suﬃcient to 
prevent the sound levels emanating from the Project from exceeding the 
maximum sound levels set forth above.  Design speciﬁcations for the 
required sound mitigation will be provided to the Town before building 
permit approval. 
b. Post-Development Sound Study 
• A post-construction noise study will be performed to document 
sound levels emanating from the Project during peak operation of 
the Project’s mechanical equipment measured at the Property line 
adjacent to residential or other noise-sensitive land uses, as 
determined by the Zoning Administrator.  The post-construction 
noise study must demonstrate that sound levels do not exceed the 
maximum sound levels set forth above.  The post-construction 
noise study will occur prior to issuance of a certiﬁcate of 
occupancy or certiﬁcate of completion, whichever occurs ﬁrst. 
Annual sound study results will be provided to the Town within 30 days 
of the anniversary of a certiﬁcate of occupancy being issued for the 
Project each year for a period of ﬁve years. 
 
2. Building Placement & Orientation  
• Buildings shall orient principal facades, including visitor, staﬀ and 
administrative entrances, to primary adjacent roads. 
• Loading docks and service entries of buildings shall not face or be 
visible from public ROW.  When possible, existing buildings may be 
used to screen loading docks.  Where building locations do not oﬀer 
screening, and in the case of phased development plans, screening of 
loading docks and service entries shall be accomplished by existing or 
proposed landscaping, fencing, walls, or similarly eﬀective methods. 
• Loading docks are permitted on only one side of the building. 
• Refuse collection areas must be fully screened on all four sides. 
 
3. Generators  
• Generators and generator use must comply with all applicable state 
and federal regulations. 
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006
SECTION IV: DEVELOPMENT & DESIGN REGULATIONS           LUCKETT ROAD SOUTH  SPECIFIC PLAN 
 
 
 
 
42 
 
• If a data center uses diesel generators, only generators that meet tier 
IV or newer emissions standards are permitted. 
• Except for generator testing or commissioning activities, generator 
use is limited to backup/emergency use only. 
• Generator testing shall be limited to weekdays between 8:00 a.m. and 
5:00p.m., except in emergencies. 
 
4. Screening & Fencing  
• Use of fencing, including the use of wrought-iron, is permitted. 
• Chain-link fencing, with or without slatted inserts, and barbed wire 
fencing are prohibited along public or private street frontages. 
• Substations and mechanical equipment shall be screened from public 
and private ROW (except for private ROW that are internal to the 
Project) and from any adjacent property that is planned or zoned with 
any use other than industrial uses using one or multiple of the 
following methods of screening: 
o Existing vegetation that will remain on the property 
o Landscaping improvements constructed on the property 
o A visually solid fence, screen wall or panel, parapet wall, or 
other visually solid screen that shall be constructed of materials 
compatible with those used in the exterior construction of the 
principal building; these features shall be at least ten feet in 
height; chain link fencing with slats is not permitted to satisfy 
this requirement 
o If the substation is located visible to Luckett Road, the screening 
shall be a 10-foot masonry wall located between Luckett Road 
and the substation in a location that does not interfere with site 
drainage.   
• Ground mounted mechanical equipment is prohibited in front yards 
(i.e., setback). 
• Solid screening walls must be constructed with a design, materials, 
details, and treatment compatible with those used on the nearest 
principal facade of a building but may include perforated surfaces as 
needed for ventilation of mechanical equipment and/or facilitate site 
drainage. 
• Mechanical equipment shall be located and screened with materials 
that provide appropriate levels of noise attenuation to reduce sound 
impacts on surrounding properties.  Where adjacent to residentially 
zoned/planned properties, mechanical equipment must be screened 
on all four sides by an acoustical barrier.  Alternatively, an acoustical 
barrier may be provided around the Project perimeter rather than 
surrounding each set of mechanical equipment. 
For purposes of this section, "acoustical barrier" is deﬁned as an 
exterior solid or louvred wall containing sound-prooﬁng materials 
designed to absorb noise and protect neighboring properties from 
noise pollution, or another eﬀective mitigation measure speciﬁed by a 
qualiﬁed third-party acoustic engineer when found by the Zoning 
Administrator to provide appropriate levels of noise attenuation. 
• Electric power lines, except electrical transmission lines carrying 48 
kV or more, shall be located underground. 
 
5. Landscape Buffers  
• Agricultural and commercial zoned/planned properties: A minimum 
50-foot buﬀer.  Buﬀering may be achieved through existing mature 
landscaping or a landscaped earthen berm that screens the Property.  
Berms shall have a slope no steeper than 2:1 with a minimum height 
of four feet and planted with a minimum of 320 plant units per 100 
feet of ROW or Property line.   
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006
SECTION IV: DEVELOPMENT & DESIGN REGULATIONS           LUCKETT ROAD SOUTH  SPECIFIC PLAN 
 
 
 
 
43 
 
• Residential or mixed-use zoned/planned properties: A minimum 100-
foot buﬀer.  Buﬀering may be achieved through existing mature 
landscaping or a landscaped earthen berm that screens the Property.  
Berms shall have a slope no steeper than 2:1 with a minimum height 
of six feet and planted and planted with a minimum of 320 plant units 
per 100 feet of ROW or Property line. 
• The preservation of existing vegetation, supplemented by new 
vegetation, if needed, or on the outside of a six-foot-tall solid fence, 
may be substituted for the above requirements when found by the 
Zoning Administrator to provide visual screening from adjacent land 
uses at the density, depth, and height equivalent to the landscape 
buﬀer with earthen berm. 
• To protect the landscaping and the preservation of open space, linear 
co-location of utilities shall not be located within the buﬀer.  Co-
location with an integrated drainage design is acceptable. 
  
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006
SECTION IV: DEVELOPMENT & DESIGN REGULATIONS           LUCKETT ROAD SOUTH  SPECIFIC PLAN 
 
 
 
 
44 
 
6. Building Design  
a. Massing & Scale 
• Buildings shall use broad, large-scale architectural gestures to provide 
variety and modulation in facade and massing as seen from public 
ROW.   
• Variation at the ground plane shall be provided to create transitions in 
scale and mass as viewed from public ROW. 
• Additive and subtractive shifts in the building footprint shall be 
provided to reduce mass and scale and to provide outdoor amenity 
spaces for employees and visitors. 
b. Principal Façade 
The following facade requirements apply to all principal façades, which 
are building façades that face adjacent existing or planned public roads 
or that face property that is planned or zoned for any use other than 
industrial uses. 
• Principal facades must incorporate diﬀerentiated surfaces at 
horizontal linear intervals that may vary in frequency, including: 
o Fenestration; and 
o A change in one of the following design elements: 
 Building material; 
 Pattern; 
 Texture; 
 Color; or 
 Accent materials. 
• Principal facades must include building step-backs or recesses, which 
shall be a minimum of two feet in depth. 
• When a building has more than one principal facade, the principal 
façades must be consistent in terms of design, materials, details, and 
treatment. 
c. Fenestration 
• Each principal facade must include fenestration comprising at least 
20% of the total surface coverage area of the principal facade. 
• The fenestration must be compatible with the other design, materials, 
details, and treatment used on the same principal facade. 
d. Main Building Entryways 
• A data center building must include a main entrance feature that is 
diﬀerentiated from the remainder of the building façade by a change 
in building material, pattern, texture, color, or accent material.  The 
entrance feature must also either project or recess from the adjoining 
building plane. 
e. Exterior Colors & Materials 
• Primary building facades shall use a neutral color palette and avoid 
high-contrast colors. 
• Accent colors shall be selected to complement the dominant building 
color, and any color change shall occur where changes in the building 
plane or recesses are provided. 
• Colors shall not act as advertisements or billboards. 
• Building exteriors shall use materials with texture and character . 
• Changes in materials shall be reﬂected in massing or oﬀsets.  The 
number of disparate materials shall be limited to a maximum of three 
primary materials to avoid a busy appearance. 
• Design elements shall be used to enhance the overall expression of 
datacenter buildings, with an emphasis on the pedestrian experience, 
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006
SECTION IV: DEVELOPMENT & DESIGN REGULATIONS           LUCKETT ROAD SOUTH  SPECIFIC PLAN 
 
 
 
 
45 
 
particularly at entryways.  All buildings shall include at least ﬁve of the 
following architectural features: 
o Overhang 
o Canopy or portico 
o Recesses or projections 
o Arcade 
o Raised corniced parapets over the entrance 
o Tower elements 
o Variation in the roof line 
 
E. Parking  
Parking will be provided in accordance with MTC Chapter 17-9 (Parking).   
 
F. Signage  
Signage will be designed and permitted in accordance with MTC Chapter 17-10 
(Signs).  The Applicant will process a sign package at time of development. 
Address and unit number signage will be incorporated per Town Addressing 
requirements. 
 
G. Vegetation & Landscaping  
1. Native Plant Protection  
Per MTC § 17-11-5 (Native Plant Protection).   
 
2. Landscape Requirements  
Per MTC § 17-11-8 (Landscape Standards for Oﬀ Street Parking Areas). 
Landscape buﬀers provided per Table IV.C and Subsection IV.D.5, above. 
 
H. Lighting  
This Project is committed to “Dark Skies” principles and the Town’s Outdoor 
Lighting Code (“OLC”), which requires shielded lighting that does not spill over 
onto adjacent properties.  A lighting plan will be prepared and submitted with 
the Project’s building permit plans in accordance with the OLC’s permit 
submission requirements. 
 
 
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006

46 
 
V. IMPLEMENTATION & ADMINISTRATION 
  
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006
SECTION V: IMPLEMENTATION & ADMINISTRATION           LUCKETT ROAD SOUTH  SPECIFIC PLAN 
 
 
 
 
47 
 
A. Administration & Interpretation  
The Speciﬁc Plan shall be administered by the Town’s Zoning Administrator, 
and all implementation decisions shall be based on the purpose of the Speciﬁc 
Plan.  If a conﬂict arises between the Speciﬁc Plan and the MTC, the Speciﬁc 
Plan shall control.  If the Speciﬁc Plan is silent on any issue, and the MTC is 
consulted, the purpose and intent of the Speciﬁc Plan shall control the Zoning 
Administrator’s decision whether and how to apply the MTC.  Appeals of any 
Zoning Administrator interpretation of this Speciﬁc Plan may be made to the 
Board of Adjustment within 15 days of the date of the interpretation.  The 
Speciﬁc Plan will not result in the modiﬁcation or change of any existing Town-
adopted building codes. 
All subsequent land use and zoning entitlements, including plats and 
development plan submittals, shall be processed in accordance with 
established Town development review procedures. 
 
B. Implementation Responsibilities  
The Master Developer is the entity responsible for ensuring the basic 
infrastructure facilities, including sewer, water and roads, are planned and 
constructed to serve the development areas within the Speciﬁc Plan in a 
timely and coordinated manner.   
Annual sound study results shall be provided to the Town within 30 days of the 
anniversary of a certiﬁcate of occupancy being issued for Project. 
C. Amendments  
Amendments to the Speciﬁc Plan are categorized as either administrative 
amendments or substantial amendments.   
The Zoning Administrator may approve administrative amendments, as 
deﬁned in MTC § 17-4-15(D), after written application is made.   
Substantial amendments to the Speciﬁc Plan shall be those changes or 
modiﬁcations that would result in a substantial change to the Speciﬁc Plan's 
regulations or the overall intent of the Speciﬁc Plan, as deﬁned in MTC § 17-4-
15(D)(3).  Substantial amendments to the Speciﬁc Plan shall be processed in 
accordance with MTC § 17-3-1. 
 
D. Phasing  
This Project will be developed in multiple phases over a ﬁve- to seven-year 
period.  Details of Project phasing will be determined at a later date and may 
be in conjunction with the Luckett Road North Speciﬁc Plan being processed 
simultaneously with this Speciﬁc Plan.

Biological Evaluation 
Luckett Road Industrial Campus - South   1 
 
PROJECT LOCATION 
The Luckett Road Industrial Campus – South project is located along Luckett Road, south of Hardin Road, 
and west of Interstate 10 (I-10) in Marana, Pima County, Arizona (Figure 1. Vicinity Map). This Biological 
Evaluation (BE) has been prepared to support a Conditional Letter of Map Revision (CLOMR) application 
and the 314-acre site consists of privately owned lands. Land use within the site consists of agricultural 
land and associated improvements including agricultural irrigation ditches and an agricultur al pond. The 
surrounding land consists of agricultural land, a cemetery, and a solar photovoltaic (PV) facility. 
 
The project is located in Section 12 of Township 11 South, Range 10 East of the Gila and Salt River Base 
Line and Meridian and is depicted on the Red Rock, Arizona 2021  and West of Marana, Arizona 2021 
USGS 7.5-minute topographic quadrangle maps (Figure 2. USGS 7.5-Minute Quadrangle Map).  
 
Throughout this BE, the construction footprint is defined as all area s of ground disturbance ( 314 acres). 
The action area includes all areas to be affected directly or indirectly by the action and not merely the 
construction footprint. In delineating the action area, we evaluated the farthest reaching physical, chemical, 
and biotic effects of the action on the environment, focusing on, but not exclusive to, the proposed 
construction of a campus style industrial development. 
PROJECT PURPOSE 
The purpose of this project is to construct a campus style industrial development (Luckett Road Industrial 
Campus – South). 
SCOPE OF WORK 
The scope of work for this project includes:  
 
• Clear and grub vegetation onsite 
• Construct utilities 
• Construct roadway infrastructure 
• Construction of onsite buildings 
PROJECT CONSTRUCTION 
Construction phasing for the project consists of clearing and grubbing of vegetation onsite, mass grading 
and raising the site elevation with fill, installing utilities, and construction of roadway infrastructure and 
onsite buildings.  
Project Construction 
The contractor will clear/grub and mass grade the site to allow for stockpile and staging areas, installation 
of utilities, and construction of roadway infrastructure and onsite buildings. Clearing, grubbing, and grading 
are required to allow earth moving equipment such as an excavator, backhoe, bobcat, grader, bulldozer, 
front-end loader, or scraper to operate. A bulldozer, front -end loader, or excavator with claw attachment 
may be required to pick up or drag trees, shrubs, and groundcover to remove them f rom the construction 
footprint. If additional staging/stockpiling areas are required, they are the responsibility of the contractor. 
The next phase will consist of installation of utilities. Equipment such as a trencher, excavator, backhoe, or 
bobcat may be required to trench for pipe, conduit, or cables underground.

Biological Evaluation 
Luckett Road Industrial Campus - South   4 
 
The action area is defined as the construction footprint and 1,100 feet beyond in all directions ( Figure 3. 
Action Area Map). 
 
After installation of utilities, roadway infrastructure would be constructed. Constructing new roadway would 
include grading and paving with asphalt and constructing curb and gutter with heavy machinery such as 
dump trucks, pickup trucks, front -end loaders  or tractors, and a paving machine.  After roadway 
infrastructure is constructed, onsite buildings would be constructed. Anticipated equipment would include 
cranes and heavy machinery to erect building walls, an excavator, backhoe, and bobcat. 
 
Construction Footprint  
Permanent impacts are defined as ground disturbance that would convert a pervious surface to an 
impervious surface. Due to the scope of work (mass grade), the entirety of the project limits (approximately 
314 acres) would be permanently impacted. Approximately 110 acres of vegetation, primarily groundcover 
(cotton), and sparse trees and shrubs will be removed from the construction footprint during construction. 
The construction footprint is illustrated in Figure 3. Action Area Map. 
PROJECT ACTION AREA 
The action area includes all areas to be affected directly or indirectly by the action and not merely the 
construction footprint (50 CFR §402.02). In delineating the action area, we evaluated the farthest reaching 
physical, chemical, and biological effects of the action on the environment, focusing on, but not exclusive 
to the campus style industrial development. 
 
Potential effects of this action on the environment resulting from this project include loss of habitat due to 
removal of vegetation, potential degradation of suitable habitat due to the introduction of invasive plant 
species, sedimentation and associated water quality impacts from filling the agricultural pond, disruption of 
wildlife from construction noise. The action area was determined based on temporary construction noise 
which is the farthest-reaching impact.  
 
The removal of vegetation may cause loss of shelter and foraging habitat for insects, predators, and prey. 
Introduction of invasive plant species from construction equipment and activities can potentially allow for 
alteration or degradation of suitable hab itat of species. Introduction of these species could cause 
degradation of potentially suitable habitat for insects, predators, and prey. Approximately 110 acres of 
vegetation, primarily groundcover (cotton), and sparse trees and shrubs will be removed during construction 
of this project. The removal of vegetation may cause loss of shelter and foraging habitat for wildlife. 
However, suitable habitat is available adjacent to the action area. 
 
Regarding sedimentation and associated water quality impacts from earth moving activities, construction 
will disturb more than one acre; therefore, a Section 402 (Arizona Pollutant Discharge Elimination System) 
permit and a Stormwater Pollution Prevention Plan (SWPPP) will be required from the Arizona Department 
of Environmental Quality (ADEQ).   
 
Sustained noise levels produced during construction can cause disruption of wildlife use of the action area. 
The ambient noise level within the construction footprint was estimated to be approximately 55 A-weighted 
decibels (dBA) (FHWA 2017). The action area is based on the loudest piece of equipment used for the 
longest duration (a bulldozer) that will be utilized for approximately 18 months. A bulldozer, which produces 
a noise level of 85 dBA at 50 feet from the source on flat ground surface, was utilized as a point source to 
compare construction noise levels with the ambient noise produced by surrounding land uses to evaluate 
potential noise impacts to wildlife. Leq is defined as the equivalent continuous noise level measured over a 
specific time period.

Biological Evaluation 
Luckett Road Industrial Campus - South   6 
 
In defining the action area for this project, the Roadway Construction Noise Model (RCNM) was used to 
determine the distance between the construction footprint and where the construction noise would attenuate 
to ambient dBA measured from Leq, which was determined to be approximately 1,100 feet (FHWA 2006). 
Construction noise could temporarily preclude wildlife from utilizing the action area; however, suitable 
habitat is available beyond the action area. 
 
LOCATION DESCRIPTION 
The project limits are approximately 1,910 feet above mean sea level (MSL) and slope/drain west. There 
are no Outstanding Arizona Waters (OAWs) or Impaired Streams in the project limits (ADEQ 2024).  
 
A site visit was conducted on August 22, 2025, by Kimley-Horn biologists (Avery Pritchard and Emily Curci). 
Wildlife observed in the field included Cooper’s hawk ( Accipiter cooperii ), ash -throated flycatcher 
(Myiarchus cinerascens), black phoebe (Sayornis saya), red-winged black bird (Agelaius phoeniceus), blue 
grosbeak ( Passerina caerulea ), yellow -headed blackbird ( Xanthocephalus xanthocephalus ), common 
raven ( Corvus corax ), American kestrel ( Falco sparverius ), and Sonoran collared lizard ( Crotaphytus 
nebrius). Nests were not observed during field reconnaissance.  
 
According to Biotic Communities, Southwestern United States, the action area is within the Lower Colorado 
Subdivision of the Sonoran Desertscrub biotic community (Data Basin 2012). The action area is situated 
west of I -10, north of the Tucson Mountains, ea st of the Silver Bell Mountains, and west of the Tortolita 
Mountains. The terrain slopes west throughout out the project limits and is relatively flat. The predominant 
geological features along the action area includes Holocene river alluvium (0 -10 ka) (US GS 2025). The 
following soils occur within the construction footprint: Gila loam, 0 to 1 percent slopes; Grabe silty clay loam; 
Glendale loam, and Pima silty clay (USDA 2025). 
 
The action area consists of agricultural land, a solar PV facility, and a cemetery. Beyond the action area 
lands are primarily additional agricultural land. 
 
An agricultural pond is the primary aquatic feature within the action area. The agricultural pond and irrigation 
ditches are manmade and used for agricultural irrigation. No perennial or semi -perennial streams are 
present within the action area. See Figure 3. Action Area Map  for representative photographs of the 
construction footprint, action area, and surrounding landscape.  
 
Information from the Arizona Department of Water Resources (ADWR) was reviewed for water wells located 
within the vicinity of the action area. According to well registration records, groundwater exists at 
approximately 115 feet below ground surface (ADWR 2 025). However, groundwater levels can fluctuate 
due to seasonal variations, groundwater withdrawal or injection and other factors. 
 
The construction footprint is located within the action area and includes agricultural fields and an agricultural 
pond. The majority of  the construction footprint consists of active and fallow cotton agricultural fields.  
Dominant vegetation observed within the agricultural pond in the northwest corner of the site included velvet 
mesquite (Prosopis velutina), salt cedar ( Tamarix ramosissima), desertbroom ( Baccharis sarothroides), 
Russian thistle (Salsola tragus), and Johnson grass (Sorghum halepense). Within the northwest corner of 
the site, the canopy primarily consists of salt cedar with a density of 50%. The subcanopy consists of 
desertbroom ranging from 10-15% density. Groundcover ranges from 0-90% with and consists of Johnson 
grass near areas with surface water.  Approximately 110 acres of vegetation, primarily cotton and sparse 
trees and shrubs, will be removed during construction.

ENDANGERED SPECIES ACT (ESA) LISTED SPECIES  
SPECIES IDENTIFICATION 
Kimley-Horn obtained an official species list from the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (USFWS) Information 
for Planning and Consultation (IPaC) system on July 30, 2025. The list included nine threatened, 
endangered, or candidate species that should be evaluated. A qualified biologist reviewed the list to 
determine species that may occur in the project vicinity. One species shown in Table 1 has the potential to 
occur in the project area and is analyzed in detail in Species Evaluation.

Life History 
Species Description and Ecology 
The adult monarch butterfly is characterized by bright orange wings surrounded by a black border with 
white spots and covered with black veins (USFWS, 2024). There are two migratory populations in North 
America, separated by the Rocky Mountains: the eastern migratory population and the western migratory 
population. 
Monarch butterflies are thought to have four life stages: egg, larva, pupa, and adults. Larva emerge from 
eggs after two to five days. Larva pupates into a pupa, which emerges 6 to 14 days later as an adult 
butterfly. Multiple generations of adult butterflies are produced during the breeding season, typically living 
two to five weeks, while overwintering migratory adults live six to nine months (USFWS, 2024).  
Monarch butterflies migrate to Arizona in the spring from overwintering sites in California and Mexico. They 
follow the blooming of nectar plants to the north and from low to higher elevations and back between spring 
and fall. While the majority of monarch  butterflies return to overwintering sites in the fall, some monarch 
butterflies remain in sheltered low-elevation riparian areas in central and southern Arizona during the winter 
months (USFWS, 2024). 
Adult monarch butterflies eat nectar from blooming nectar plants. Common desert blooming nectar plants 
with high numbers of monarch butterflies observed across their range in Arizona include rubber rabbitbrush 
(Ericameria nauseosa), lateflowering thoroughwort (Eupatorium serotinum), common sunflower (Helianthus 
annuus), and golden crownbeard ( Verbesina encelioides ) (The Xeres Society for Invertebrate 
Conservation, 2018). Eggs and larval monarch butterflies are milkweed obligates; larvae feed on milkweed 
to sequester toxic cardenolides as a defense against predators (USFWS, 2024). 
Reproduction 
Monarch butterfly breeding season in Arizona typically occurs from early spring to early fall. Monarch 
butterflies migrating to overwintering sites in the fall enter reproductive diapause (suspended reproduction) 
and break reproductive diapause before migr ating away from their overwintering sites in February and 
March. As they migrate to cooler climates, monarch butterflies breed and their offspring continue to disperse 
further north until they reenter reproductive diapause in the fall and begin the cycle of generational migration 
over again (USFWS, 2024).  
Suitable Habitat  
Monarch butterflies can be found throughout Arizona where blooming nectar plants are available. They also 
require milkweed to lay eggs and develop as larvae. Nectar and milkweed resources are often associated 
with riparian corridors but are found throughou t Arizona (Western Monarch Milkweed Mapper, 2017). 
Suitable overwintering sites protect monarch butterflies from the elements and maintain moderate 
temperatures that are warm enough to prevent freezing yet cool enough to prevent lipid depletion. In 
Arizona, monarch butterflies may overwinter in low desert washes (USFWS, 2024). 
 
Threats 
Primary threats to monarch butterfly include habitat loss, continued exposure to insecticides, and effects of 
climate change. Urban, agricultural, and industrial development have resulted in significant loss and 
degradation of the species’ habitat. Other factors causing the loss or alteration of monarch butterfly habitat 
include intensive herbicide usage for weed control which has resulted in widespread milkweed eradication. 
Additional threats to the species include disease and parasitism: specifically, a p rotozoan parasite 
Ophryocystis elektroscirrha, which decreases survival and fitness in the monarch (USFWS, 2024). 
Range and Survey History 
Monarch butterfly populations have been declining at overwintering sites in both the Eastern and Western 
migratory populations. Non -migratory populations inside and outside of North America are considered 
extant. Milkweed and monarch have been documented t hroughout Arizona (Western Monarch Milkweed 
Mapper, 2017). Based on available data, the current range and distribution for the species is largely the

same as was historically documented. No population extirpations or range reductions have been 
documented in Arizona (USFWS, 2024). 
Habitat Suitability Evaluation  
The construction footprint and action area contain vegetation suitable as food or stopover habitat, including 
desertbroom and velvet mesquite. According to the Western Monarch Mapper, the nearest documented 
breeding monarch butterfly observation is over 16  miles from the construction footprint and the nearest 
documented milkweed occurrence is over 12 miles away from the construction footprint (Western Monarch 
Milkweed Mapper, 2017). 
Analysis and Determination of Effects  
Impacts: Potential impacts to monarch butterfly from this project would include removal of suitable stopover 
and foraging habitat. 
Approximately 110 acres of vegetation, primarily cotton and sparse trees and shrubs,  will be removed 
during construction of this project. Nectar plants observed during field reconnaissance include velvet 
mesquite and desert broom. Approximately 1 percent of plants within the project area are nectar producing 
plants. No milkweed was observed within the construction footprint.  
To summarize, the construction footprint contains suitable stopover and foraging habitat that will be 
removed. However, there is suitable stopover and foraging habitat beyond the construction footprint within 
the action area and beyond. Therefore, impacts are discountable. 
 
No potential for "Take" exists (meaning that the project has no potential to harass, harm, pursue, hunt, 
shoot, wound, kill, trap, capture, or collect, or to attempt to engage in any such conduct) to monarch 
butterfly.

Western Burrowing Owl (Athene cunicularia hypugaea) 
 
The western burrowing owl is listed as a species of concern by the USFWS and a Tier 2 SGCN by the 
AGFD. They are also protected federally by the MBTA and Arizona State Law (ARS Title 17). The western 
burrowing owl utilizes well drained grasslands, steppes,  deserts, prairies, and agricultural lands, often 
associated with burrowing mammals. Western burrowing owls are known to occupy vacant lots near human 
habitation, golf courses, and/or airports (AGFD 2022).  
 
According to AGFD’s habitat suitability model, on average 99% of the habitat within the project limits is 
potentially suitable for the western burrowing owl. During field reconnaissance, no western burrowing owls 
were observed; however, suitable burrows an d suitable habitat (primarily along agricultural ditches) are 
present within the project limits. Therefore, mitigation measures will be required.  
 
Since there is a high probability for burrowing owls to occur onsite, we recommend conducting an initial 
protocol survey ~120 days prior to construction.  Assuming owls are observed, we recommend obtaining a 
project-specific permit from USFWS to allow for relocation of owls ~30 days prior to construction.  Lastly, 
we recommend a final preconstruction survey less than 30 days from construction to ensure owls have not 
inhabited the site post-relocation. 
 
Developer Responsibilities: 
 
• The Developer will employ a biologist to complete a pre-construction survey for burrowing 
owls prior to construction in all suitable habitats that will be disturbed. The biologist shall 
possess a burrowing owl survey protocol training certificate issued by  the Arizona Game 
and Fish Department. 
 
• Prior to construction, all personnel who will be on -site, including, but not limited to, 
contractors, Contractors’ employees, supervisors, inspectors, and subcontractors shall 
review the attached “Western Burrowing Owl Awareness” flyer. 
 
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006
 
 
Contractor Responsibilities (continued): 
• If any burrowing owls or active burrows are identified the contractor shall notify the 
Developer immediately. No construction activities shall take place within 100 feet of any 
active burrow. 
• If the Developer in cooperation with the Biologist determines that burrowing owls cannot be 
avoided, the contractor shall employ a qualified biologist holding a permit from the U.S. Fish 
and Wildlife Service to relocate burrowing owls from the project area, as appropriate.  
PROTECTED NATIVE PLANTS 
Protected native plants within the project limits include velvet mesquite. Native plants will be removed as 
part of the project; therefore, the following mitigation measure should be implemented. 
 
Contractor Responsibility: 
 
• Protected native plants within the project limits may be impacted by this project; therefore, 
the Developer will determine if Arizona Department of Agriculture notification is needed. If 
notification is needed, the Developer will send the notification at least 60 (sixty) calendar  
days prior to the start of construction.  
MIGRATORY BIRD TREATY ACT 
Migratory birds may nest on the ground, on structures, or in trees, shrubs, or other vegetation within the 
project limits. No nests were observed during the site visit; however, trees, shrubs, and groundcover 
suitable for nesting will be removed. Therefore, the following mitigation measure should be implemented: 
 
Contractor Responsibility: 
 
• If vegetation clearing occurs during the migratory bird breeding season (March 1 ‐ August 
31), the contractor shall avoid any active bird nests. If the active nests cannot be avoided, 
the contractor shall notify the Engineer to evaluate the situation. During the non ‐breeding 
season (September 1 ‐ February 28) vegetation removal is not subject to this restriction.

Disclaimer:
1. This Environmental Review is based on the project study area that was entered. The report must be
updated if the project study area, location, or the type of project changes.
2. This is a preliminary environmental screening tool. It is not a substitute for the potential knowledge
gained by having a biologist conduct a field survey of the project area. This review is also not intended to
replace environmental consultation (including federal consultation under the Endangered Species Act),
land use permitting, or the Departments review of site-specific projects.
3. The Departments Heritage Data Management System (HDMS) data is not intended to include potential
distribution of special status species. Arizona is large and diverse with plants, animals, and
environmental conditions that are ever changing. Consequently, many areas may contain species that
biologists do not know about or species previously noted in a particular area may no longer occur there.
HDMS data contains information about species occurrences that have actually been reported to the
Department. Not all of Arizona has been surveyed for special status species, and surveys that have been
conducted have varied greatly in scope and intensity. Such surveys may reveal previously
undocumented population of species of special concern.
4. Arizona Wildlife Conservation Strategy (AWCS), specifically Species of Greatest Conservation Need
(SGCN), represent potential species distribution models for the State of Arizona which are subject to
ongoing change, modification and refinement. The status of a wildlife resource can change quickly, and
the availability of new data will necessitate a refined assessment. 
Locations Accuracy Disclaimer:
Project locations are assumed to be both precise and accurate for the purposes of environmental review. The
creator/owner of the Project Review Report is solely responsible for the project location and thus the correctness
of the Project Review Report content.

Project Type Recommendations:
During the planning stages of your project, please consider the local or regional needs of wildlife in regards to movement,
connectivity, and access to habitat needs. Loss of this permeability prevents wildlife from accessing resources, finding
mates, reduces gene flow, prevents wildlife from re-colonizing areas where local extirpations may have occurred, and
ultimately prevents wildlife from contributing to ecosystem functions, such as pollination, seed dispersal, control of prey
numbers, and resistance to invasive species. In many cases, streams and washes provide natural movement corridors
for wildlife and should be maintained in their natural state. Uplands also support a large diversity of species, and it is
important to identify and conserve upland wildlife movement corridors. In addition, maintaining biodiversity and
ecosystem functions can be facilitated through improving designs of structures, fences, roadways, and culverts to
promote passage for a variety of wildlife species. Guidelines for many of these can be found at:
https://www.azgfd.com/wildlife-conservation/planning-for-wildlife/planning-for-wildlife-wildlife-friendly-guidelines/.
Consider impacts of outdoor lighting on wildlife and develop measures or alternatives that can be taken to increase
human safety while minimizing potential impacts to wildlife.  Artificial lighting could impair the ability of nocturnal animals
to navigate (e.g., owls, migratory birds, bats, and other nocturnal mammals) and may affect wildlife behavior and
populations. The AZGFD recommends using only the minimum amount of light needed for safety, especially in areas
immediately adjacent to open space or undeveloped lands. The AZGFD encourages the use of motion sensing lighting
and narrow spectrum lighting (amber or warm tones typically 2700 Kelvin or lower) wherever possible to lower the range
of species affected by lighting. Also, please consider shielding, canting, or cutting all lighting, where possible, to ensure
that light reaches only areas needing illumination and to minimize impacts to nocturnal wildlife.
Page 10 of 12
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006
Arizona Game and Fish Department project_report_luckett_road_industrial_cam_89977_92706.pdf
Project ID: HGIS-25747 Review Date: 7/30/2025 11:54:27 AM
Minimize the potential introduction or spread of exotic invasive species, including aquatic and terrestrial plants, animals,
insects and pathogens. Precautions should be taken to wash and/or decontaminate all equipment utilized in the project
activities before entering and leaving the site. See the Arizona Department of Agriculture website for a list of prohibited
and restricted noxious weeds at https://www.invasivespeciesinfo.gov/  and the Arizona Native Plant Society
https://aznps.com/invas for recommendations on how to control these species. To view a list of documented invasive
species or to report invasive species in or near your project area visit iMapInvasives - a national cloud-based application
for tracking and managing invasive species at https://imap.natureserve.org/imap/services/page/map.html. 
To build a list: zoom to your area of interest, use the identify/measure tool to draw a polygon around your area of
interest, and select “See What’s Here” for a list of reported species. To export the list, you must have an
account and be logged in. You can then use the export tool to draw a boundary and export the records in a csv
file. 
 
Evaluate potential impacts to wildlife and fish species due to changes in access to water, water quality, quantity,
chemistry, temperature, and alteration to flow regimes (timing, magnitude, duration, and frequency of floods). Minimize
impacts to springs, in-stream flow, and consider irrigation improvements to decrease water use. If dredging is a project
component, consider timing the project to minimize impacts to spawning fish and other aquatic species. Wash, drain, and
dry equipment to reduce the spread of exotic invasive species. AZGFD recommends early coordination with the Project
Evaluation Program (PEP@azgfd.gov) for projects that could impact water resources, wetlands, streams, springs, and/or
riparian habitats.
The AZGFD recommends that wildlife surveys are conducted to determine if noise-sensitive species, such as birds or
mammals, occur within the project area. Avoidance or minimization measures could include conducting project activities
outside of breeding seasons.
If trenching or digging of large holes is necessary, the AZGFD recommends trenching/digging and backfilling crews be
close together to minimize the amount of open holes at any given time. Where trenches or holes cannot be back-filled
immediately, the AZGFD recommends escape ramps be constructed in each hole and at least every 90 meters in
trenches. Escape ramps can be short lateral trenches or wooden planks sloping to the surface. The AZGFD
recommends that slopes be less than 45 degrees (1:1) and trenches and holes that have been left open be inspected to
remove animals prior to backfilling.
Communities can actively support the sustainability and mobility of wildlife by incorporating wildlife planning into their
regional/comprehensive plans, their regional transportation plans, and their open space/conservation land system
programs. An effective approach to wildlife planning begins with the identification of the wildlife resources in need of
protection, an assessment of important habitat blocks and connective corridors to be preserved, and the incorporation of
these critical wildlife components into community plans and programs. Community planners should also work with State
and local transportation planning entities, and planners from other communities, to foster coordination and cooperation in
developing compatible development plans to ensure wildlife habitat connectivity. The AZGFD's guidelines for
incorporating wildlife considerations into community planning and developments can be found on the Wildlife Friendly
Guidelines portion of the Wildlife Planning page at https://www.azgfd.com/wildlife-conservation/planning-for-
wildlife/planning-for-wildlife-wildlife-friendly-guidelines/.
The AZGFD recommends development plans  include  open, natural space to facilitate wildlife movement, while also
minimizing the potential for wildlife-human interactions through design features. Please reference the Department’s
Wildlife Friendly Guidelines for community and project planners at  https://www.azgfd.com/wildlife-conservation/planning-
for-wildlife/planning-for-wildlife-wildlife-friendly-guidelines/. Coordination with the Project Evaluation Program
(PEP@azgfd.gov or 623-236-7600) is recommended for project specific recommendations to maximize wildlife
permeability and minimize human-wildlife conflicts.

This review has identified riparian areas within the vicinity of your project. During the planning stage of your project,
avoid, minimize, or mitigate any potential impacts to riparian areas identified in this report. Riparian areas play an
important role in maintaining the functional integrity of the landscape, primarily by acting as natural drainages that convey
water through an area, thereby reducing flood events. In addition, riparian areas provide important movement corridors
and habitat for fish and wildlife. Riparian areas are channels that contain water year-round or at least part of the year.
Riparian areas also include those channels which are dry most of the year, but may contain or convey water following
rain events. All types of riparian areas offer vital habitats, resources, and movement corridors for wildlife. The Pinal
County Comprehensive Plan (i.e. policies 6.1.2.1 and 7.1.2.4), Open Space and Trails Master Plan, Drainage Ordinance,
and Drainage Design Manual all identify riparian area considerations, guidance, and policies. Guidelines to avoid,
minimize, or mitigate impacts to riparian habitat can be found
at https://www.azgfd.com/wildlife-conservation/planning-for-wildlife/planning-for-wildlife-wildlife-friendly-guidelines/.
Further consultation with the Arizona Game and Fish Department and Pinal County may be warranted.

APPENDIX D  
Western Burrowing Owl Awareness 
Flyer 
 
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006
Western Burrowing Owl Awareness 
The purpose of this flyer is to provide contractors 
working on projects with basic knowledge to reduce the 
risk of incidental take of Western Burrowing Owls. 
Legal Status: 
Western Burrowing Owls (Athene cunicularia) are protected under the Federal Migratory Bird Treaty Act 
of 1918. All migratory birds and their parts are fully protected. They are also protected under Arizona 
State Law in Title 17‐101, Title 17‐235, and Title 17‐236. 
What to look for: 
• Description– small, ground‐dwelling owl.
• Length– 19.5‐25.0 cm (7.68‐9.85 inches)
• Wingspan– 58.42 cm (23.0 inches)
• Mass– about 150 grams
• Males are typically slightly larger than females.
• Round head, lacks ear tufts.
• Distinct oval facial ruff, framed by a broad, puffy
white eyebrow.
• Eyes contain a bright yellow iris.
Identifying an active burrow: 
• Owls use burrows constructed by ground squirrels, badgers, coyotes and tortoises. They can also
use pipes, culverts, and ditches.
• Presence of excrement (whitewash) near entrance to burrow.
• Burrowing owls frequently decorate entrance of burrows with cow or horse manure, feathers,
vegetation and trash items.
How to avoid them: 
• Scan ahead prior to arriving at a sign location.
• If burrowing owls are observed within the project area, stop and move at least 100 feet beyond
the owl or occupied burrow before resuming work.
If you think your work may potentially impact a Burrowing Owl or active burrow, please stop. 
Move at least 100 feet from the animal or burrow before resuming work. 
Source: Arizona Game and Fish Department Animal Abstract: Western Burrowing Owl. Heritage Data Management System 
Where are owls found? 
• Dry, open, short grass, treeless plains.
• Dependent on fossorial mammals.
(ground squirrels, prairie dogs, badgers,
etc.) to construct burrows.
• Human dominated landscapes: golf
courses, airports, agricultural fields.

Executive Summary 
HDR performed a preliminary desktop code review and pre-construction background noise 
monitoring for the proposed Luckett Road Industrial Campus - South Project.  
To determine existing background noise levels at nearby noise-sensitive receivers, HDR performed 
24-hour noise measurements at three locations proximal to Project site boundaries. The results of 
these measurements were used to inform Project ambient noise. 
The Lmax represents the highest root-mean-square sound pressure level during a given time period. 
The L90 level represents the sound level exceeded for 90 percent of a given time period. As such, 
the L90 level is generally considered a reasonable metric for describing the background noise level in 
absence of temporary elevated noise events such as transient vehicle passbys or plane flyovers.  
The highest hourly L90 level was 55 dBA at Measurement Location 2 (ML2) during the late night, 
while the lowest hourly L90 was 27 dBA, which occurred during the daytime at ML1.  
HDR identified a single existing noise sensitive area (NSA) within 1,000 feet of the Project site. This 
is the Arizona Veteran’s Memorial Cemetery at Marana, which is located approximately 150 feet 
northeast of the Project site in the Town of Marana, and zoned residential. 
The nearest identified residences are 15426 W Hardin Road, located 0.8 miles southwest in 
unincorporated Pima County land, zoned for Rural Homestead, and 14975 N Luckett Road, located 
0.9 miles south within the Town of Marana, zoned for single family residential.  
Zoning in adjacent unincorporated areas of Pinal and Pima counties are also subject to their 
respective county’s noise limits. Site planning should be coordinated with the various authorities 
having jurisdiction on established noise limits, planned land uses, and active permits.
