1 Introduction 
Luckett Road Industrial Campus – South (the Project) is a proposed data center facility 
planned to be constructed in the Town of Marana, Arizona. Although Marana spans 
portions of both Pima and Pinal Counties, the Project site is located in Pima County, as 
shown in Figure 1. HDR has performed a preliminary environmental noise desktop review, 
along with noise monitoring of existing environmental background noise conditions for the 
Project (Appendix A). 
Figure 1. Site Location in Marana, Arizona 
 
2 Acoustic Concepts 
Sound is comprised of tiny fluctuations in air pressure and characterized by parameters 
such as amplitude, frequency, and duration. Within the range of human hearing, sound 
can vary in amplitude by more than 1 million units. Therefore, a logarithmic scale, known 
as the decibel (dB) scale, is used to quantify sound intensity and compress the scale to a 
more manageable range. The terms “sound” and “noise” (unwanted sound) are used 
interchangeably in this report. 
The human ear does not hear all frequencies equally, as the hearing organs of the inner 
ear de-emphasize low and very high frequencies. The A-weighting scale is the most 
common weighting scale used to represent this selective sensitivity of human hearing to 
sound pressure levels. It places more emphasis or “weight” on the frequencies we hear 
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006
Luckett Road Industrial Campus - South 
Final Acoustic Report- Update  
2 | August 29, 2025  
 
effectively, and less weight on frequencies we do not hear effectively. A-weighted 
decibels are noted using the abbreviation dBA. Being a decibel quantity, root-mean-
square (RMS) sound pressure levels are referenced to 20 µPa, the accepted threshold of 
human hearing. Table 1 lists typical A-weighted sound pressure levels for some common 
noise sources.  
Table 1. Typical Source Noise Levels 
Sound Pressure Level, dBA Typical Sources 
90 Motorcycle at 25 feet 
Gas lawn mower at 3 feet 
80 Garbage disposal 
70 City street corner 
Vacuum cleaner at 10 ft. 
60 Conversational speech 
50 Typical office 
40 Living room (without television) 
30 Quiet bedroom at night 
20 Approximate threshold of hearing 
dBA = A-weighted sound pressure level  
Source: Minnesota Pollution Control Agency, 2015 [5] 
Because of the logarithmic scale, sound levels cannot be simply added or subtracted. If 
sound energy is doubled, the sound pressure level only increases by 3 dB. However, a 
doubling of sound energy is not perceived by humans as a doubling of loudness. A 3-dB 
change is considered a “just noticeable” difference, a 5-dB change is considered a 
“clearly noticeable” difference, and a 10-dB change is considered a perceived doubling 
or halving of loudness. 
Most sounds are made up of a wide range of frequencies and termed broadband sounds. 
Sounds that are focused within a particular narrow frequency range are tonal sounds. 
Sound sources can be constant or time-varying. Environmental sound levels are often 
expressed over periods of time, allowing time-varying signals to be represented by sound 
levels averaged over intervals (e.g., a 1-hour period). One metric used to describe 
environmental sound is the equivalent continuous sound pressure level (Leq). The Leq 
represents a constant sound that, over the specified time period, has the same acoustic 
energy as the time-varying signal. Another descriptor is Ln, which is the noise level 
exceeded “n” percent of the time. For example, the L90 is the noise level exceeded 90 
percent of a given time. For a 1-hour period, this would represent the noise level 
exceeded for 54 minutes during that hour. The Lmax and Lmin represent the highest and 
lowest RMS sound pressure levels during a given time period, respectively.  
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006
Luckett Road Industrial Campus - South 
 Final Acoustic Report- Update  
 
August 29, 2025 | 3 
 
3 Preliminary Code Review 
HDR conducted a preliminary desktop review of national, regional, and local authority 
requirements regarding noise trespass and permissible facility noise levels to 
surrounding land uses. To the extent possible, noise ordinances and guidance applicable 
to standard data center operations, focusing on mechanical equipment noise emissions, 
routine generator exercising and maintenance, and emergency generator usage were 
identified. Note that code review efforts are focused on facility operations; construction 
noise limits, vibration limits, and other non-operational noise emissions which are beyond 
the scope of this review.  
3.1 Federal Environmental Noise Requirements 
Applicability of federal environmental noise requirements has been considered for the 
Project but deemed not applicable. In their summary of the Noise Control Act (42 U.S.C. 
§4901 et seq. (1972), the USEPA states “primary responsibility for control of noise rests 
with State and local governments”, with Federal action being “essential to deal with major 
noise sources in commerce, control of which require national uniformity of treatment” [2]. 
As a private commercial venture, federal funds or property are understood to be not 
affiliated with the Project. Note that the USEPA’s 1974 Information on Levels of 
Environmental Noise Requisite to Protect Public Health and Welfare with an Adequate 
Margin of Safety [3] document provides guidance of 55 dBA (Ldn or 24-hour Leq) and 
45 dBA (Ldn or 24-hour Leq) depending on specific application for outdoor and indoor 
areas, respectively.  The Day-Night Average Sound Level, or Ldn, is a weighted 24-hour 
average sound level with a 10 dBA “penalty” added to noise that occurs at night to better 
account for increased noise sensitivity associated with those hours. 
3.2 State Environmental Noise Requirements 
While Arizona does not have a designated statewide noise ordinance, Title 13 Chapter 
2903 (ARS 13-2903) Disorderly conduct classification of the Arizona Revised Statutes 
(ARS) [1], codifies criminal conduct related to unreasonable noise as follows: 
A. A person commits disorderly conduct if, with intent to disturb the peace or quiet 
of a neighborhood, family or person, or with knowledge of doing so, such person: 
2. Makes unreasonable noise. 
Aside from disorderly conduct laws for unreasonable noise, State of Arizona statutes 
also apply to vehicle exhaust systems (ARS 28-955), shooting ranges (ARS 17-602), 
sound attenuation building standards in the vicinity of miliary airports (ARS 28-8482), and 
others, but data center noise emissions are not specifically addressed. Environmental 
quality prescripts within the Arizona Administrative Code address some industrial 
applications such as water resources, mining operations, and worker noise limits, but do 
not appear to apply to data center operations. Noise policies pertaining to ADEQ are 
understood to follow those established by the USEPA within the Noise Control Act of 
1972 [2].

Luckett Road Industrial Campus - South 
Final Acoustic Report- Update  
4 | August 29, 2025  
 
3.3 County Environmental Noise Requirements 
The Project is located in the Town of Marana, Arizona, with the nearest portions of 
unincorporated Pinal County located approximately 1/2-mile to the north, and 
unincorporated portions of Pima County located along the western property boundary. 
3.3.1 Pima County 
Chapter 9.30 – Regulation of Excessive, Unnecessary and Annoying Noises of the Pima 
County, Arizona Code of Ordinances [6] governs unincorporated areas of the county, 
including adjacent receiving properties to the west, northwest, and southwest of the site. 
Because the Project is located within the incorporated boundaries of the Town of 
Marana, specific applicability of the Pima County ordinance is unclear. If applicable, such 
as at receiving properties, Pima County provides subjective, annoyance-based prescripts 
for regulating noise in Section 9.30.040 Impermissible sound levels, as follows: 
In addition to the prohibited noises described in 9.30.050, 9.30.060 and 
9.30.070, it shall be unlawful for any person to make or continue, or cause or 
permit to be made or continued, any excessive, unnecessary or offensive noise 
which disturbs the peace or quiet of any neighborhood or which causes 
discomfort or annoyance to any reasonable person of normal sensitivity residing 
in the area. 
Sections 9.30.050, 9.30.060, and 9.30.070 referenced in the above ordinance excerpt 
address noise emissions from loud radios/sound sets, vehicular noise, and construction, 
respectively. Section 9.30.080 Exemptions does not specifically exempt emergency 
generator operation. 
Although the receiving parcel adjacent to the western property line, extending also to the 
northwest and southwest of the Project parcel, is presently a solar energy installation, 
current zoning maps designate this land use as an RH Rural Homestead Zone parcel. 
Title 18 Zoning of the Pima County, Arizona Code of Ordinances sets forth zoning 
requirements for various types of land uses. Chapter 18.13 RH Rural Homestead Zone 
provides for “commercial and industrial development only where appropriate and 
necessary to serve the needs of the rural area” but does not appear to prescribe specific 
noise related zoning requirements.  
3.3.2 Pinal County 
Chapter II – Noise of the Pinal County, Arizona Code of Ordinances [7] governs 
unincorporated areas of the county, including potential non-adjacent receiving properties 
as close as approximately 1/2-mile north of the site. Within 1 mile of the site, 
unincorporated Pinal County land uses include properties zoned as various industrial and 
industrial buffer areas (CI-1, I-1, I-2, and I-3), commercial (C-3), rural (GR), and activity 
center (AC-3) uses. Because the Project is located within the incorporated boundaries of 
the Town of Marana, specific applicability of the Pinal County ordinance is unclear. If 
applicable, Pinal County provides subjective, annoyance-based prescripts for regulating 
noise in Section 10-21 - Excessive noise violation, as follows: 
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006
Luckett Road Industrial Campus - South 
 Final Acoustic Report- Update  
 
August 29, 2025 | 5 
 
It shall be unlawful and a violation of this chapter for any person to knowingly 
and/or intentionally make, continue, or cause to be made or continued, or to 
allow or permit any excessive noise as defined in section 10-22. 
Additionally, Section 10-24 – Acts or conditions declared as excessive noises also 
provides quantitative sound pressure level limits for various land uses from which the 
noise is emitted. However, if the receiving property has a zoned land use with a lower 
noise limit, the lower of the two limits must be achieved. These limits are expressed in 
terms of a 2-minute Leq in dBA. Table 2 shows these limits. Presuming industrial, 
commercial, and rural receiving property land use applications within 1 mile of the Project 
parcel, Leq lower limits of 65 dBA and 60 dBA for a given 2-minute interval during daytime 
and nighttime hours, respectively, may apply. It should be noted that per zoning 
descriptions, noise-sensitive uses, such as educational institutions, hospitals, childcare 
centers, churches, offices, assisted living facilities, and similar, could be included within 
these zoning areas.

Noise level limits for activity center zoning district, AC-3, are unclear based on Table 2 
information. This zoning district is described for use in areas with a comprehensive plan 
designation of high intensity activity center core, designed to create a vibrant urban 
center, while allowing for “some less intense uses in the short term.” As with the 
previously mentioned industrial, commercial, and rural zoning districts, listed AC-3 
zoning district applications can include noise-sensitive land uses. The most stringent 
noise limits in all circumstances, however, are reserved for residential zoning districts, as 
listed in Table 2. 
Section 10-27 Exemptions does not specifically exempt emergency generator operation. 
3.4 Local Noise Ordinance Requirements 
Being within the incorporated Town of Marana limits, facility operations are governed by 
the Marana Town Code [8]. Chapter 11-5. Noise of this code regulates noise. Similar to 
previously discussed county codes, it qualitatively limits nuisance noise from loud radios 
and sets construction start/stop times. Additionally, it prohibits causing false alarms in the 
town and sets quantitative noise limits applying to properties within Resort and 
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006
Luckett Road Industrial Campus - South 
Final Acoustic Report- Update  
6 | August 29, 2025  
 
Recreation zoning. Because no Resort and Recreation zoned properties exist near the 
Project site, this portion of the noise code is deemed not applicable to the Project.  
Receiving properties adjacent to the north, east, and south property lines are zoned for 
single-family residential. Specific plan uses understood to be approved for residential 
homes largely east of I-10, agricultural, light industrial, medium lot, small lot, and 
transportation corridor uses are included in non-adjacent land use applications within the 
general vicinity. Small and medium lot uses may span residential, agricultural, 
commercial, and industrial applications. 
Section 17-6-13 Data Centers of Chapter 17-6. General and Miscellaneous Development 
Regulations additionally regulates data centers within the town, with Paragraph G – 
Noise Attenuation providing specific prescripts in the regulation of data center noise 
generation. This paragraph provides quantitative noise limits for data centers, based on 
the receiving property’s zoning, to be measured at the receiving property line. These 
limits are based on the maximum sound level, or Lmax, in dBA, which are listed in 
Table 3. 
Note that the code defines “Daytime” as the hours between 7:00AM and 7:00PM on 
weekdays, or between 9:00AM and 8:00PM on Saturdays, Sundays, and legal holidays 
observed by the town. “Nighttime” is defined in the code as between 7:00PM and 
7:00AM on weekdays, or between 8:00PM and 9:00AM on Saturdays, Sundays, and 
legal holidays observed by the town.  
Table 3. Maximum Sound Levels for Data Center Operation, Town of Marana  
Planned or Zoned Use  
Affected Property 
Maximum dBA Daytime/ 
 Nighttime 
Residential and mixed-use 55/55 
Agricultural 60/55 
Commercial 65/60 
Industrial 72/65 
Source: Town of Marana Code, 2025 [9] 
This portion of the ordinance states that generators and generator use must comply with 
all applicable state and federal regulations and if a data center uses diesel generators, 
only Tier IV or newer generators are permitted. Except for generator testing or 
commissioning activities, generator use is limited to backup/emergency use only and 
limited to weekdays between 8:00AM and 5:00PM, except in emergencies. However, 
noise emissions from the generators are not specifically excluded, noting that the limits in 
Table 3 apply to “any noise which emanates from any operation, activity, or source on a 
data center site, including, but not limited to, heating and cooling system(s).” 
The ordinance states that “the data center must be designed and built to incorporate 
sound mitigation methods sufficient to prevent the sound levels emanating from the data 
center, as determined by a qualified third-party acoustic engineer, from exceeding the 
maximum sound levels set forth” and “design specifications for the required sound 
mitigation must be provided to the town before building permit approval.”  
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006
Luckett Road Industrial Campus - South 
 Final Acoustic Report- Update  
 
August 29, 2025 | 7 
 
Further, the data center specific portion of the noise code specifies a noise study must 
be conducted to document baseline sound levels in the area of the proposed data center 
and the expected noise levels generated by the facility. Moreover, a post-construction 
noise study must be performed to document noise levels measured at the nearest 
property line during peak operation of facility equipment prior to issuance of a certificate 
of occupancy or certificate of completion to demonstrate Project compliance with 
maximum allowable noise level limits. Such post-construction compliance measurements 
are to occur annually for 5 years after completion and upon the town’s request thereafter. 
HDR recently completed the pre-construction background noise measurement portion of 
code requirements and is presented herein. Other requirements are beyond HDR’s 
scope at this time. 
3.5 Code Summary 
Due to numerous jurisdictions bordering the Project site, it is challenging to determine 
which specific ordinances, particularly to receiving properties located in jurisdictions 
other than the proposed Project, are applicable to the Project. It is therefore 
recommended these jurisdictions be engaged during the development process to clarify 
and confirm the full extent of Project requirements. The ordinances with quantitative 
noise limits include the Pinal County code, as well as the specific data center section 
(Section 17-6-13) of the Town of Marana code. In addition, sections of the Pima County 
and Pinal County ordinances place subjective, nuisance-based limitations on noise. 
Issues of acoustical annoyance are complex and can be influenced by many factors, 
including loudness perception, low frequency noise content, time of day operations, 
relative noise level increases, and noise character (i.e., tonal content). 
3.6 Land Use Applications 
Zoning and land use application information is presented in this section. 
3.6.1 Zoning and Land Uses 
To identify nearby noise-sensitive areas (NSAs), HDR used aerial imagery, in 
combination with Pima County, Pinal County, and the Town of Marana zoning and parcel 
data.  
For reference, Figure 2 shows the zoning data of Pinal County for non-adjacent parcels 
beginning 1/2-mile from the Project site, extending to 1 mile from the Project site. 
Figure 3 and Figure 4 show zoning data for unincorporated Pima County and Town of 
Marana parcels within 1 mile of the Project site, respectively. Figure 5 delineates noise-
sensitive areas within proximity of the Project site. 
Unincorporated Pinal County parcels within relative proximity of the Project site (at or 
greater than 1/2-mile) are a combination of industrial or industrial buffer areas (CI-1, I-1, 
I-2, and I-3), commercial (C-3), rural (GR), and activity center (AC-3) uses, although 
zoning use descriptions permit potential noise-sensitive applications (i.e., educational 
institutions, hospitals, childcare centers, churches, offices, assisted living facilities, and 
similar) in these zoning districts. No residences were identified in the vicinity based on 
current zoning and aerial observations. The nearest residential equivalent is an RVP

Marana, with adjacent properties to the east, north, and south identically zoned within the 
incorporated town limits. Specific plan uses understood to be approved for residential 
homes largely east of I-10, agricultural, light industrial, medium lot, small lot, and

zoned for rural homestead use, while 14975 N Luckett Rd is zoned for single family 
residential use. 
Noise-producing land uses in the vicinity of the Project include the nearby Pinal Air Park 
(a small commuter airport), which generated audible noise at the proposed facility site 
during HDR’s noise monitoring visit. Serrano Solar, a solar farm, Nelson Drilling (a 
drilling company), and PNP Materials (a construction company) are also shown in the 
area according to Google Maps, but did not appear to create any subjectively noticeable 
noise to HDR attendants during the site noise monitoring.

3.6.3 Potential Risk Assessment 
The only identified noise sensitive area within 1,000 feet of the Project site is Arizona 
Veteran’s Memorial Cemetery at Marana. This cemetery is located approximately 
150 feet from the edge of the Project site and is separated from the Project by N Luckett 
Road. Figure 5 shows this location.  
Due to the presence of a noise-sensitive area within 500 feet or less of the data center 
property boundary, the Project should be considered Critical until noise modeling is 
completed. The most restrictive noise limits of these codes are the nighttime residential 
noise limits, which set forth a 55 dBA limit. However, these codes define both the limiting 
noise metric and the daytime and nighttime periods differently. The Pinal County code 
(potentially applying to more distant properties from the Project site) defines its noise 
limits in terms of a 2-minute Leq in dBA and defines nighttime at residences as being from 
8:00 PM to 7:00 AM. The Town of Marana code defines its noise limits in terms of an 
Lmax in dBA and defines nighttime during the weekday as between 7:00 PM and 7:00 AM.  
Note that the most restrictive noise limit for both codes is for residentially zoned 
properties. The nearest residence which has been identified is approximately 0.8 miles 
from the site boundary. However, residentially zoned parcels, including directly adjacent 
to the Project, have been identified closer. Unincorporated Pinal County parcels are at 
least approximately 1-half mile from the Project site.  
Due to the various jurisdictional boundaries involved (one town and two counties), along 
with the disparity between (less noise-sensitive) existing land use applications compared 
to (more noise-sensitive) zoning, some level of uncertainty exists in definitively identifying 
noise level limits and applying noise codes. Therefore it is recommended the Project 
team work to determine noise code applications, which may be part of any potential 
rezoning process or as Project development in general, issues that are difficult to discern 
within the parameters of a desktop review.  
With the exception of the existing cemetery at the northeast corner of the site, design of 
the facility to current residential zoning noise limits where noise-sensitive residences do 
not currently exist could lead to unnecessary expenditures; conversely, designing the 
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006
Luckett Road Industrial Campus - South 
Final Acoustic Report- Update  
14 | August 29, 2025  
 
facility to less noise-sensitive land use limits affiliated with present land uses could lead 
to future unexpected noise control expenditures that may be very challenging to 
implement post-construction, if residential allowance were realized. Site planning should 
be coordinated with the various authorities having jurisdiction on established noise limits, 
planned land uses, and active permits. 
In addition to the aforementioned zoning and land use characterization, both Pima and 
Pinal County noise ordinances place subjective, nuisance-based limitations on noise 
emissions where issues of acoustical annoyance, such as low frequency noise content, 
time of day operations, relative noise level increases, and noise character (i.e., tones), 
may arise. Once again, such consideration should be taken in the context of existing 
noise-generating surrounding land uses and nearby transportation corridors, including 
already present tonal noise emissions from surrounding solar equipment. Table 4 
presents an acoustic risk matrix.

4 Noise Measurements 
To document existing background noise levels at the proposed Project site, HDR 
performed 24-hour unattended noise monitoring at three representative locations within 
the Project boundaries. Noise monitoring locations were selected near noise-sensitive 
receivers as pertinent, as well as areas where the environmental soundscape of the 
Project may differ due to neighboring noise-generating property uses. Figure 6 shows the 
measurement locations, Project boundaries, and nearest locations of interest. 
The measurement locations ML1 and ML3 were selected to obtain 24-hour noise levels 
representative of nearby known noise-generating and noise-sensitive locations of 
interest. ML2 was selected to obtain 24-hour background noise levels adjacent to 
residential zoned parcels to the south, although current land uses are not noise-
sensitive.  
Figure 6. Measurement Locations

• ML1

o Ended Wednesday, July 30, 2025, at 8:51 AM

measurement periods, when HDR staff were still onsite, were excluded from each set of 
measurement data due to noise generated by equipment set-up, disassembly, and 
checks not affiliated with actual environmental background noise levels.

4.1 Instrumentation 
HDR used a digital sound level meter with 1/3 octave band filters to perform the noise 
measurements. The sound level meter and handheld calibrator meet Class 1/Type 1 
precision requirements of ANSI and International Electrotechnical Commission 
standards. All instrumentation used to measure noise levels on this Project is calibrated 
on a regular basis by an independent accredited calibration laboratory using standards 
traceable to the National Institute of Standards and Technology. The instrumentation was 
adjusted to a reference level traceable to the National Institute of Standards and 
Technology prior to transportation to the measurement site. Calibration checks were 
performed in the field prior to and upon completion of each measurement. Measurement 
logs and photos for each location are included in Appendix B.  
The equipment operated continuously at each measurement location. The meter 
recorded sound level data at 1-second intervals to aid in identifying transient events that 
could affect results. Additionally, the meter collected weather data including wind speed, 
direction, and precipitation, also at 1-second intervals. The meter recorded sound level 
data at a height of approximately 5 feet above grade (industry-accepted standard 
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006
Luckett Road Industrial Campus - South 
Final Acoustic Report- Update  
18 | August 29, 2025  
 
listening height) and approximately 10 feet from any reflective surfaces. All sound 
measurement metrics were captured with a fast time constant. 
Weather in the form of precipitation and high winds (typically defined as winds in excess 
of 11 miles per hour [mph]) can create spurious noise, contaminating measurement 
results. During these measurements, no precipitation events were recorded.  
However, wind speeds at or in excess of 11 mph were recorded for a total of 97 minutes 
over the course of the measurement period. Sampling periods during which wind speeds 
greater than or equal to 11 mph occurred have been removed from analysis.  
The periods during which most of these wind speed exceedances occurred are as 
follows: 
• At ML1, 3:00 PM to 7:00 PM on Tuesday, July 29, 2025 
• At ML2, 8:00 PM to 9:00 PM on Wednesday, July 30, 2025 
• At ML3, 3:00 PM to 4:00 PM on Thursday, July 31, 2025 
4.2 Results 
For the analysis, daytime hours are from 7:00 AM to 7:00 PM, while nighttime hours are 
from 7:00 PM to 7:00 AM, based on the Town of Marana’s designations for weekday 
limits on data center noise. In addition, for reference purposes, the period from 12:00 AM 
to 5:00 AM will be analyzed as a “quietest hours” designation. Figure 7, Figure 8, and 
Figure 9 provide graphical summaries of the hourly Leq measurement results at ML1, 
ML2, and ML3, respectively.  
Note that certain periods of measurement did not include an entire hour of analyzed data 
due to removal of recorded data which occurred while HDR staff were on-site. This 
occurred during the following time periods: 
• Tuesday, July 29, 2025, from 8:00 AM to 8:50 AM 
• Wednesday, July 30, 2025, from 8:40 AM to 9:45 AM 
• Thursday, July 31, 2025, from 9:55 AM to 10:35 AM 
• Friday, August 1, 2025, from 10:30 AM to 11:00 AM

At ML1, the primary source of noise was traffic from the nearby I-10 Highway to the 
northeast. Additionally, commuter airplane flyovers from the nearby Pinal Air Park, 
northwest of the property, were noticeable on-site and upon audio review. Birdsong and 
bug chirping was also audible, with birdsong being most active in the early daytime, and 
bug chirping during the nighttime.  
At ML2, noise was dominated by a nearby electrical equipment box used to control 
pumps to the irrigation canals in the field. Noise generated from water babbling from a 
nearby irrigation canal was also present throughout the measurement period, which 
became dominant when pump flow increased. Similar to ML1, traffic noise from the 
nearby I-10 Highway and commuter airplane flyovers from the nearby Pinal Air Park also 
contributed. 
Of the three measurement locations, traffic noise from the nearby I-10 Highway was the 
most dominant at ML3, due to its proximity to this roadway. As such, this source 
dominated most of the measurement period at this location. Commuter airplane flyovers 
from the nearby Pinal Air Park were also noticeable. Insect chirping was also active 
during the nighttime.  
A summary of results at the three measurement locations is provided in Table 5. Results 
are given as the overall average Leq, L90, Lmax, and Lmin for the daytime, nighttime, and 
late-night periods. Additionally, Table 6 presents the maximum and minimum Leq, L90, 
Lmax, and Lmin for the daytime, nighttime, and late-night periods, for each measurement 
location. Periods with high wind speeds are excluded from these results.

The L90 represents the sound level exceeded for 90 percent of a given time period. As 
such, it is generally considered a reasonable metric for describing the background noise 
level in the absence of temporary elevated noise events such as transient vehicle 
passbys, or plane flyovers.  
The average nighttime and late night L90 values at ML1 and ML3 are higher than the 
daytime values, which implies that the background noise levels during the nighttime at 
these locations are louder than those during the daytime. The loudest hourly L90 was 
found to be 55 dBA at ML2 during late night, while the quietest hourly L90 was found to 
be 27 dBA, which occurred during the daytime at ML1.

APPENDIX F: Acoustic Modeling Results 
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006
Luckett South
Preliminary Modeling Results
September 29, 2025
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006
Regulations
• The data center noise ordinance in the Town of Marana Code of Ordinance indicates that the 
noise levels are “measured at the property line of the nearest property to the data center 
property that is planned or zoned for residential land uses, or other noise sensitive use as 
reasonably determined by the zoning administrator, during peak operation of the data center 
mechanical equipment.” Per the Town of Marana GIS, the surrounding parcels are zoned as 
“Single Family Residential”.
• Noise emanating from the Project will not exceed the maximum permissible sound levels for the 
affected adjacent use per Town ordinance. The Town has identified this Property and surrounding 
properties as appropriate for Employment uses in its long-range plans. Although the property to 
the north is zoned residential, it is also expected to be developed with an industrial use.
• The limits specific to data centers are:
Planned or zoned use of affected property Maximum dBA Daytime/Nighttime
Residential and mixed-use 55/55
Agricultural 60/55
Commercial 65/60
Industrial 72/65
Exhibit A to Resolution No. 2026-006
Mitigation Options
• Quieter rooftop fans, mitigated inlet louvers, and mitigated exhausts 
were used in the “Mitigation” scenarios. These solutions impact the 
normal mechanical operations scenario primarily, with some benefit 
for the scenarios that also have generators running. 
• Noise barrier walls or quieter generator enclosures around the 
generator yards can further reduce noise during the maintenance and 
emergency scenarios but are not modeled in the following graphics.
